\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\nu F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)}

Note that
F
-algebras should not be confused with Eilenberg-Moore algebras of
monads (as we indicated above, those satisfy additional laws).

We generally drop sub- and superscripts, e.g. on natural
transformations, whenever this improves readability.

3.
Abstractly Guarded Categories and Monads

The notion of guardedness is paramount in process algebra: typically
one considers systems of mutually recursive process definitions of the
form
x_{i}=t_{i}
, and a variable
x_{i}
is said to be guarded in
t_{j}

if it occurs in
t_{j}
only in subterms of the form
a.\,s
where

a.\,(\operatorname{-\!-})
is action prefixing. A standard categorical approach
is to replace the set of terms over variables
X
by an object
TX

where
\mathbb{T}
is a monad. We then can model separate variables by
partitioning
X
into a sum
X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}
and thus talk about
guardedness of a morphism
f\colon X\to T(X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n})
in any
X_{i}
,
meaning that every variable from
X_{i}
is guarded in
f
. One way to capture guardedness categorically is to identify the
operations of
\mathbb{T}
that serve as guards by distinguishing a suitable
subobject of
TX
; e.g. the definition of completely iterative
monad
[
23
]
follows this approach. For our purposes, we
require a yet more general notion where we just distinguish some
Kleisli morphisms as being guarded in certain output variables. We
thus aim to work in a Kleisli category of a monad, but since our
formalization and initial results can already be stated in any
co-Cartesian category, we phrase them at this level of generality as
long as possible.

Definition 3.1

(Abstractly guarded
category/monad)
.

A co-Cartesian category
\mathbf{C}
is

abstractly guarded
if it is equipped with a notion of

abstract guardedness
, i.e. with a relation between morphisms

f\colon X\to Y
and summands
\sigma\colon Y^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Y
closed under the rules in
Figure
1
where
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}Y
denotes the fact that

f
and
\sigma
are in the relation in question.

A monad is
abstractly guarded
if its Kleisli category is
abstractly guarded. A monad morphism
\alpha\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
between
abstractly guarded monads
\mathbb{T}
,
\mathbb{S}
is
abstractly guarded

if
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}TY
implies
\alpha f\colon X\to_{\sigma}SY
.

\displaystyle\textbf{(trv)}\quad\frac{f\colon X\to Y}{~\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\,f\colon X\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}Y+Z~}\qquad\qquad\textbf{(par)}\quad\frac{~f\colon X\to_{\sigma}Z~\quad{}~g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}Z}{~[f,g]\colon X+Y\to_{\sigma}Z}

\displaystyle\textbf{(cmp)}\quad\frac{~f\colon X\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}Y+Z\qquad g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}V\qquad h\colon Z\to V~}{[g,h]\,f\colon X\to_{\sigma}V}

Figure 1
.
Axioms of abstract guardedness.

The rules in Figure
1
are designed so as to
enable a reformulation of the classical laws of iteration w.r.t. abstract guardedness, as we shall see in Section
5
.
Intuitively,
(trv)
states that if a program does
not output anything via a summand of the output type then it is
guarded in that summand. Rule
(par)
states that putting two guarded equation systems
side by side again produces a guarded system. Finally,
rule
(cmp)
states that guardedness is preserved under
composition: if the unguarded part of the output of a program is
postcomposed with a
\sigma
-guarded program, then the result is

\sigma
-guarded, no matter how the guarded part is
transformed. That is, guardedness, once introduced, cannot be “undone”
through sequential composition, but it can be “forgotten”, as
the following
weakening rule
indicates:

\textbf{(wkn)}\quad\frac{~f\colon X\to_{\sigma}Y~}{~f\colon X\to_{\sigma\theta}Y~},

where
\sigma
and
\theta
are composable summands. This rule was originally
part of our axiomatization
[
19
]
but it was
later observed to be derivable from the other three
[
17
]
:

Proposition 3.2
.

Rule
(wkn)
is derivable in the calculus of Figure
1
.

Proof 3.3
.

Let
\bar{\sigma}\colon Z\to Y
be the complement of
\sigma
, thus

Y=Z+Y^{\prime}
,
\sigma=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
and
\bar{\sigma}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
. Analogously we
present
Y^{\prime}
as
Z^{\prime}+Y^{\prime\prime}
with
\theta=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
. In summary,
Y
is a
coproduct of
Z
,
Z^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
,
f\colon X\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}Z+(Z^{\prime}+Y^{\prime\prime})
, and
we need to show that
f\colon X\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}Z+(Z^{\prime}+Y^{\prime\prime})
. Since

f=[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f
, by
(cmp)
we are left to check that

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\colon Z\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}Z+(Z^{\prime}+Y^{\prime\prime})
. Now
Z+(Z^{\prime}+Y^{\prime\prime})
is also a
coproduct of
Z+Z^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}
, with evident injections; so

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\colon Z\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}Z+(Z^{\prime}+Y^{\prime\prime})
is equivalent to

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\colon Z\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}(Z+Z^{\prime})+Y^{\prime\prime}
, which is an instance
of
(trv)
.

Rule
(wkn)
is a weakening principle: If a program
is guarded in some summand then it is guarded in any subsummand of
that summand. Analogously, we obtain stability of guardedness under isomorphisms:

Proposition 3.4
.

The rule

\textbf{(iso)}\quad\frac{~f\colon X\to_{\sigma}Y\qquad h\colon Y\mathbin{\cong}Z}{~h\,f\colon X\to_{h\sigma}Z~},

is derivable in the calculus of Figure
1
.

Proof 3.5
.

Let
\bar{\sigma}\colon W\to Y
be the complement of
\sigma
, thus

Y=W+Y^{\prime}
,
\sigma=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
and
\bar{\sigma}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
. Now,
Z
is a coproduct of
W
and
and
Y^{\prime}
with
h\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\colon W\to Z
and
h\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\colon Y^{\prime}\to Z
as the coproduct injections, and

h
is the copair of
h\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
and
h\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
w.r.t. this coproduct structure. The
rule in question now follows from
(cmp)
, using the fact that by
(trv)
,

h\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
is
h\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded.

We write
f\colon X\to_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}
as a
shorthand for
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}
with

\sigma=[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{i_{1}},\ldots,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{i_{k}}]\colon X_{i_{1}}+\ldots+X_{i_{k}}\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}X_{1}+\ldots+X_{n}
.
More generally, we sometimes need to refer to components of some

X_{i_{j}}
. This amounts to replacing the corresponding
i_{j}
with a
sequence of pairs
i_{j}n_{j,m}
, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{i_{j}}
with

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{i_{j}}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n_{j,1}},\ldots,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{n_{j,k_{j}}}]
, so, e.g. we
write
f\colon X\to_{12,2}(Y+Z)+Z
to mean that
f
is

[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]
-guarded. Where coproducts
Y+Z
etc. appear
in the rules, we mean any coproduct, not just some selected coproduct.

Recall that we have defined the notion of guardedness as a certain
relation between morphisms and summands. Clearly, the
greatest

such relation is the one declaring all morphisms to be

\sigma
-guarded for all
\sigma
. We call categories (or monads)
equipped with this notion of guardedness
totally guarded
. It
turns out we also always have a
least
guardedness relation
(originally called
trivial

[
19
]
):

Definition 3.6

(Vacuous guardedness)
.

A morphism
f\colon X\to Y
is
vacuously

\sigma
-guarded
for
{\sigma\colon Z\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Y}
if
f
factors through
the coproduct complement
\bar{\sigma}
of
\sigma
.

Intuitively,
f
is vacuously guarded in
{\sigma\colon Z\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Y}

if
f
does not output anything via the summand
Z
; observe that by
the
(trv)
rule, vacuous guardedness always implies
guardedness. Formally, we have:

Proposition 1
.

By taking the abstractly guarded morphisms to be the vacuously
guarded morphisms, we obtain the least guardedness relation making
the given category into a guarded category.

Proof 3.7
.

As indicated above, it is immediate from
(trv)
that every
vacuously guarded morphism is guarded under any guardedness relation
making the category into a guarded category. It remains to show that
vacuous guardedness is closed under the rules in
Figure
1
; in the following we write

f\colon X\to_{\sigma}Y
to mean that
f
is vacuously
\sigma
-guarded.

•

(trv)
: Immediate from the definition of vacuous
guardedness.

•

(cmp)
: Suppose
f\colon X\to_{2}Y+Z
, i.e.
f=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\,w
for some
w\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\colon X\to Y
. Now, for

g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}V
and
h\colon Z\to V
,

[g,h]\,f=g\,w
. Let
\bar{\sigma}\colon W\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}V
be the
complement of
\sigma\colon V^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}V
. By assumption,
g
factors through

\bar{\sigma}
, i.e.
w=\bar{\sigma}\,u
for some
u
. Therefore

[g,h]\,f=\bar{\sigma}\,u\,w
, which by definition
means that
[g,h]\,f
is vacuously
\sigma
-guarded.

•

(par)
: Suppose that
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}Z
and
g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}Z
,
i.e.
f=\bar{\sigma}\,f^{\prime}
and
g=\bar{\sigma}\,g^{\prime}
for some
f^{\prime}\colon X\to Z^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}\colon Y\to Z^{\prime}

where
\sigma\colon Z^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Z
and
\bar{\sigma}
is the coproduct complement of
\sigma
. Then, of course,

[f,g]=\bar{\sigma}\,[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}]
, i.e.
[f,g]\colon X+Y\to_{\sigma}Z
.
∎

We call a guarded category (or monad)
vacuously
guarded
if its notion of abstract guardedness is given by vacuous
guardedness. We note briefly how vacuous guardedness instantiates to
Kleisli categories:

Lemma 2
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a monad on a category
\mathbf{C}
. A morphism

f\colon X\to T(Y+Z)
is vacuously
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded iff
f
factors through

T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
in
\mathbf{C}
.

Proof 3.8
.

Immediate from the fact that the left injection into the coproduct

Y+Z
in the Kleisli category of
\mathbb{T}
is
\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
, and

(\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})^{\star}=T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
.

The notion of abstract guardedness can thus vary on a large
spectrum from
vacuous guardedness
to
total guardedness
,
possibly detaching it from the initial intuition on guardedness. It is
for this reason that we introduced the qualifier
abstract
into
the terminology; for brevity, we will omit this qualifier in the
sequel in contexts where no confusion is likely, speaking only of
guarded monads, guarded morphisms etc.

Remark 3
.

One subtle feature of our axiomatization is that it allows for
seemingly counterintuitive situations when a morphism is
individually guarded in two disjoint summands, but not in their
union. This can be illustrated by the following example. Let
\mathbb{T}

be the algebraic monad induced by the theory of abelian groups
presented, in additive notation, by binary
-
alone. In this
presentation, the zero element is presented by terms of the form

x-x
; the theory thus differs slightly from the more standard
presentation in that there is no zero element in the absence of
variables, i.e.
T\iobj=\iobj
. We equip
\mathbb{T}
with vacuous
guardedness. Now let
z:1\to T(\{x\}+\{y\})
be the map that picks
out the zero element. This morphism is both
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
-guarded and

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded, i.e. it factors both through
T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
and through

T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
, since we can write the zero element both as
y-y
and as

x-x
. However,
z
fails to be
\mathsf{id}
-guarded, because it does not
factor through
T\iobj=\iobj
.

Note that, conversely, collective guardedness does always imply
individual guardedness, for by
(wkn)
,
f\colon X\to_{1,2}Y+Z

implies both
f\colon X\to_{1}Y+Z
and
f\colon X\to_{2}Y+Z
.

As usual, guardedness serves to identify systems of
equations that admit solutions according to some global principle:

Definition 3.9

(Guarded (pre-)iterative category/monad)
.

Given
f\colon X\to_{2}Y+X
, we say that
f^{\dagger}\colon X\to Y
is a

solution
of
f
if
f^{\dagger}
satisfies the
fixpoint
identity

f^{\dagger}=[\mathsf{id},f^{\dagger}]\,f
. A guarded category
is
guarded pre-iterative
if it is equipped with an

iteration operator
that assigns to every
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded
morphism
f\colon X\to_{2}Y+X
a solution
f^{\dagger}
of
f
. If every such

f
has a unique solution, we call the category
guarded
iterative
.

A guarded monad is
guarded (pre-)iterative
if its Kleisli
category is guarded
(pre-)
iterative. A guarded monad morphism

\alpha\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
between guarded pre-iterative monads

\mathbb{T},\mathbb{S}
is
iteration-preserving
if

\alpha f^{\dagger}=(\alpha f)^{\dagger}
for every
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
.

We can readily check that the iteration operator preserves
guardedness:

Proposition 4
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
be a guarded pre-iterative category, let
\sigma\colon Z\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Y
, and let
f\colon X\to_{\sigma+\mathsf{id}}Y+X
. Then

f^{\dagger}\colon X\to_{\sigma}Y
.

Proof 3.10
.

Let
\bar{\sigma}\colon Z^{\prime}\to Y
be the complement of
\sigma\colon Z\to Y
, so we proceed under
the assumption that
Y=Z^{\prime}+Z
,
\sigma=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
and
\bar{\sigma}=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
. Then

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle\;[\mathsf{id},f^{\dagger}]f=[[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}],f^{\dagger}]\,f=[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2},f^{\dagger}]]\,[[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}],\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f.

By assumption,
f\colon X\to_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}+\mathsf{id}}(Z^{\prime}+Z)+X
. The morphism

h=[[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}],\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]
is simply an associativity isomorphism, for which

h\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}+\mathsf{id})=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
, hence by
(iso)
,

[[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}],\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f\colon X\to_{2}Z^{\prime}+(Z+X)
. Since by
(trv)
,

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded, we are done by
(cmp)
.

We note that for guarded morphisms into guarded

iterative
monads, preservation of iteration is automatic:

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
be a guarded morphism between guarded
pre-iterative monads
\mathbb{T}
,
\mathbb{S}
with
\mathbb{S}
being guarded
iterative. Then
\alpha
is iteration-preserving.

Proof 3.12
.

Indeed, given
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
,

\displaystyle\alpha f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\;=\alpha\,[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}f

/​​/ fixpoint identity for
f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\;=[\eta,\alpha f^{\dagger}]^{\star}\alpha f

/​​/ monad morphism

but this equation has
(\alpha f)^{\dagger}
as its unique solution,
hence
(\alpha f)^{\dagger}=\alpha f^{\dagger}
.

In vacuously guarded categories, there is effectively
nothing to iterate, so we have

Proposition 5
.

Every vacuously guarded category is guarded iterative.

Proof 3.13
.

Let
f\colon X\to_{2}Y+X
, which by assumption means that
f=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}g
for
some
g
. Then for any
f^{\dagger}
satisfying

f^{\dagger}=[\mathsf{id},f^{\dagger}]\,f
, we have

f^{\dagger}=[\mathsf{id},f^{\dagger}]\,f=[\mathsf{id},f^{\dagger}]\,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}g=g
,
which proves uniquenes of solutions. Moreover,

[\mathsf{id},g]f=[\mathsf{id},g]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}g=g
, which shows existence.

We now revisit our motivating considerations on process
algebra from the beginning of this section.

Example 3.14

(Generalized processes)
.

A natural semantic domain for finitely branching possibly infinite
processes under strong bisimilarity with final results in
X
and
atomic actions in
A
is the final coalgebra

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}(X+A\times\gamma)
in the category of sets and
functions, where
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}
is the finite powerset
monad. Alternatively, we can view inhabitants of this domain as
equivalence classes of possibly non-well-founded terms over
variables from
X
, which can also be thought of as process names,
and over the operations
+
of non-deterministic choice, deadlock

\iobj
and action prefixing
a.\,(\operatorname{-\!-})
. The latter view is
useful for syntactic presentations of those processes that happen to
be finite. Systems of recursive process definitions are naturally
represented by morphisms

f\colon X\to\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}((Y+X)+A\times\gamma)
where
X
contains
process names being defined and
Y
contains the remaining process
names that can occur freely. For example, the system

\displaystyle x=y+a.\,x

(2)

corresponds to the following data:

X=\{x\}
,
Y=\{y\}
,
A=\{a\}
, and

\displaystyle f(x)=

\displaystyle\,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}y,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\}\rangle\}

(eliding the isomorphism
Y+X\cong\{x,y\}
). The generalization
arising from this example is as follows: Given an endofunctor
\Sigma

on a co-Cartesian category
\mathbf{C}
and a monad
\mathbb{T}
such that final
coalgebras
T_{\Sigma}X=\nu\gamma.\,T(X+\Sigma\gamma)
exist, we
obtain a corresponding monad
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
called the

generalized coalgebraic resumption monad transform
of

\mathbb{T}
. As above, we can view morphisms
f\colon X\to T_{\Sigma}(Y+X)
as
systems of recursive equations for
generalized processes
with

\mathbb{T}
capturing the relevant computational effect (such as
non-determinism) and
\Sigma
capturing
atomic steps
(such as
actions
\Sigma=A\times\operatorname{-\!-}
).

Abstract guardedness can be used to effectively distinguish
those systems
f\colon X\to T_{\Sigma}(Y+X)
for which we can define

desirable
solutions
f^{\dagger}\colon X\to T_{\Sigma}Y
. For the
moment, we proceed under the assumption that
desirable
means

unique
, for instance (
2
) has the unique solution

x=y+a.\,(y+a.\,(\ldots))
. Let us recall the existing approach
to defining guardedness in this context via completely iterative
monads
[
23
]
, which are based on idealized
monads
[
23
, Definition 5.5]
. To make this precise, recall
some definitions.

Definition 3.15

(Monad modules, idealized monads)
.

A
module
over a monad
\mathbb{T}
on
\mathbf{C}
is a pair
(M,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\circ})
, where
M
is an endomap over the objects of
\mathbf{C}
,
while the lifting
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\circ}
is a map
\mathsf{Hom}(X,TY)\to\mathsf{Hom}(MX,MY)
such that the following laws are satisfied:

\displaystyle\eta^{\circ}=\mathsf{id},

\displaystyle g^{\circ}f^{\circ}=(g^{\star}f)^{\circ}.

Note that
M
extends to an endofunctor by taking

Mf=(\eta f)^{\circ}
. A
module-to-monad morphism

is a natural transformation
\xi\colon M\to T
that satisfies

\xi f^{\circ}=f^{\star}\xi
. We call the tuple

(\mathbb{T},M,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\circ},\xi)
an
idealized
monad
; when no confusion is likely, we refer to these data just
as
\mathbb{T}
. An
idealized monad morphism
between idealized monads

((T,\eta^{T},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\star}),M,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\circ},\xi)

and

((S,\eta^{S},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\text{\kreuz}}),N,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\bullet},\xi^{\prime})

is a pair
(\alpha,\beta)
where
\alpha\colon T\to S
is a monad
morphism while
\beta\colon M\to N
is a natural
transformation satisfying
\alpha\xi=\xi^{\prime}\beta
and

\beta f^{\star}=f^{\text{\kreuz}}\beta
.

Example 3.16
.

It follows from previous
results
[
30
, Corollary 3.13]
that the monad

\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
from Example
3.14
is idealized when
equipped with the module
T\Sigma T_{\Sigma}
. In the concrete case
where
\mathbb{T}={\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}
and
\Sigma=A\times(\operatorname{-\!-})
, i.e.
T_{\Sigma}X=\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}((Y+X)+A\times\gamma)
, the module

{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}(A\times T_{\Sigma})
contains processes that consist of
(finitely many) non-deterministic branches all of which begin with
an action.

Milius
[
23
]
defines guardedness only for
equation morphisms, i.e. morphisms of type
X\to T(Y+X)
. Extending
this notion in the obvious way to morphisms of type
X\to T(Y+Z)
as
required in our framework, we obtain the following definition:

Definition 3.17

(Completely iterative monads)
.

Given an idealized monad

(\mathbb{T},M,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\circ},\xi)
, a morphism

f\colon X\to T(Y+Z)
is
guarded
if it factors via

[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]\colon Y+M(Y+Z)\to T(Y+Z)
. The monad

\mathbb{T}
is
completely iterative
if every guarded

f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
in this sense has a unique solution.

It turns out that the above notion of guardedness is not an
instance of abstract guardedness; specifically, it does not satisfy
our
(par)
rule. Equation (
2
) provides a good
illustration of what happens: although both terms
y
and
a.\,x
are
guarded in
x
, we cannot factor the corresponding term

X\to T(Y+X)
through any

[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]\colon Y+M(Y+X)\to T(Y+X)
due to the
top-level nondeterministic choice.

Fortunately, we can fix this by
noticing that completely iterative monads actually support iteration
for a wider class of morphisms:

Definition 3.18
.

Let
(\mathbb{T},M,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\circ},\xi)
be an idealized
monad. Given
\sigma\colon Z\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Y
, we say that a morphism

f\colon X\to TY
is
weakly
\sigma
-guarded
if it factors
through
[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}\colon T(Y^{\prime}+MY)\to TY

for a complement
\bar{\sigma}\colon Y^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Y
of
\sigma
.

Since a morphism that factors as
[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]f
can be
rewritten as
[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]^{\star}\eta f
, every guarded
morphism in an idealized monad is also weakly guarded.

Theorem 3.19
.

Let
(\mathbb{T},M,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\circ},\xi)
be an idealized
monad. Then the following hold.

(1)

\mathbb{T}
becomes abstractly guarded
when equipped with weak guardedness as the notion of abstract
guardedness.

(2)

If
\mathbb{T}
is completely iterative,
then every weakly
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded morphism
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
has a
unique solution.

(3)

If
(\alpha,\beta)
is an idealized monad morphism, then
\alpha
preserves weak guardedness.

That is, completely iterative monads are abstractly guarded iterative
monads w.r.t. weak guardedness.

Proof 3.20
.

(
1
):
We need to verify that weak
guardedness is closed under the rules from Definition
3.1
.

•

(trv)

Given a morphism
f\colon X\to TY
, the following holds:

\displaystyle(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f=

\displaystyle\;(\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})^{\star}f

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})^{\star}f

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]^{\star}(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f

/​​/ Kleisli

•

(cmp)

Given
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+Z)
,
g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}TV
, and
h\colon Y\to TV
, assume that
f
factors as
[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}
, while
g
factors as
[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime}
. Then, the following holds:

\displaystyle[g,h]^{\star}f=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\star}[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\star}[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi])^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\star}\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\star}\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\star}\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},\xi[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\circ}]^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ module-to-monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\circ}]^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\circ}]^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}[g^{\prime},\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\circ}])^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}[g^{\prime},\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime},h]^{\circ}]^{\star}f^{\prime}.

/​​/ Kleisli

•

(par)

Given a morphism
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}TZ
and
Y\to_{\sigma}TZ
assume that
f
factors as
[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}
, and
g
factors as
[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime}
. Then, the following holds:

\displaystyle[f,g]=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime},[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}g^{\prime}]

/​​/ guardedness

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}[f^{\prime},g^{\prime}].

/​​/ coproducts

(
2
):
Let

f=[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\xi]^{\star}j
for a morphism

j\colon X\to T(Y+M(Y+X))
. We define an auxiliary morphism

g=[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},j]^{\star}\xi\colon M(Y+X)\to T(Y+M(Y+X))
.
Note that
g
is guarded (in the sense of
[
23
]
), since it
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{j}]^{\star}\xi=

\displaystyle\;\xi[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{j}]^{\circ}

/​​/ module-to-monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{\xi}]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{j}]^{\circ}.

/​​/ coproducts

Thus,
g
has a unique solution
g^{\dagger}\colon M(Y+X)\to TY
. We
use it to define a solution to
f
, namely

f^{\ddagger}=[\eta,g^{\dagger}]^{\star}j
. It is left to show that it
is indeed a solution and that it is unique:

•

Solution:

\displaystyle f^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}g}]^{\star}j

/​​/ solution

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{j}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{([{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{j}])^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{\xi}])^{\star}j

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{f^{\ddagger}}]^{\star}f.

•

Uniqueness: Let
r\colon X\to TY
be a solution of
f
,
that is,
r=[\eta,r]^{\star}f
. First, we calculate:

\displaystyle[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}f}]^{\star}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{\xi}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{([{\eta},{r}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{\xi}])^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{[{\eta},{r}]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j}]^{\star}\xi

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{j}])^{\star}\xi

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{j}]^{\star}\xi

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}g.

Thus,
[\eta,r]^{\star}\xi
is a solution of
g
. By uniqueness, we obtain that
g^{\dagger}=[\eta,r]^{\star}\xi
. With this, we can check the uniqueness of
f^{\ddagger}
:

\displaystyle r=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{\xi}]^{\star}j

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([{\eta},{r}]^{\star}[{\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{\xi}])^{\star}j

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{[{\eta},{r}]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{[{\eta},{r}]^{\star}\xi}]^{\star}j

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[{\eta},{g^{\dagger}}]^{\star}j

/​​/ the above

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;f^{\ddagger}.

(
3
):

Let
(\alpha,\beta)
be as in Definition
3.15
. Let
f\colon X\to TY
be weakly
\sigma
-guarded. This means that
f
factors as
[\eta^{T}\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}
for a morphism
f^{\prime}\colon X\to T(Y^{\prime}+MY)
. We need to show that
\alpha f\colon X\to SY
factors as
[\eta^{S}\bar{\sigma},\xi^{\prime}]^{\text{\kreuz}}g
for some
g\colon X\to S(Y^{\prime}+NY)
. We calculate:

\displaystyle\alpha f=

\displaystyle\;\alpha[\eta^{T}\bar{\sigma},\xi]^{\star}f^{\prime}

/​​/ factorisation of
f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\alpha[\eta^{T}\bar{\sigma},\xi])^{\text{\kreuz}}\alpha f^{\prime}

/​​/ monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\alpha\eta^{T}\bar{\sigma},\alpha\xi]^{\text{\kreuz}}\alpha f^{\prime}

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta^{S}\bar{\sigma},\alpha\xi]^{\text{\kreuz}}\alpha f^{\prime}

/​​/ monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta^{S}\bar{\sigma},\xi^{\prime}\beta]^{\text{\kreuz}}\alpha f^{\prime}

/​​/ idealized monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta^{S}\bar{\sigma},\xi^{\prime}](\mathsf{id}+\beta))^{\text{\kreuz}}\alpha f^{\prime}

/​​/ coproducts

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta^{S}\bar{\sigma},\xi^{\prime}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\eta^{S}(\mathsf{id}+\beta))^{\text{\kreuz}}\alpha f^{\prime}

/​​/ Kleisli

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta^{S}\bar{\sigma},\xi^{\prime}]^{\text{\kreuz}}(\eta^{S}(\mathsf{id}+\beta))^{\text{\kreuz}}\alpha f^{\prime}

/​​/ Kleisli

4.
Parametrizing Guardedness

Uustalu
[
35
]
defines a
parametrized monad
to be a
functor from a category
\mathbf{C}
to the category of monads over
\mathbf{C}
. We
need a minor adaptation of this notion where we allow parameters from
a different category than
\mathbf{C}
, and simultaneously introduce a
guarded version of parametrized monads:

Definition 4.1

(Parametrized guarded monad)
.

A
parametrized (guarded) monad
is a functor from a category

\mathbf{D}
to the category of (guarded) monads and (guarded) monad
morphisms over
\mathbf{C}
. Alternatively (by uncurrying), it is a
bifunctor
\mathbin{\hash}\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}
such that for any

X\in|\mathbf{D}|
,
\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}X\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
is a (guarded) monad, and
for every
f\colon Z\to V
,
\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f\colon X\mathbin{\hash}Z\to X\mathbin{\hash}V
is the

X
-component of a (guarded) monad morphism

\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}f\colon\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}Z\to\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}V
,
explicitly,

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f)\,\eta=\eta

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f)\,g^{\star}=((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f)\,g)^{\star}(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f)\,

(3)

for any
g\colon X\to Y
and, in the guarded case,

g\colon Z\to_{\sigma}V\mathbin{\hash}X\quad\text{implies}\quad(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f)\,g\colon Z\to_{\sigma}V\mathbin{\hash}Y.

A
parametrized (guarded) monad morphism
between parametrized
(guarded) monads qua functors into the category of (guarded) monads
over
\mathbf{C}
is a natural transformation that is componentwise a
(guarded) monad morphism. In uncurried notation, given parametrized
monads
\mathbin{\hash},\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}
a natural
transformation
\alpha\colon\mathbin{\hash}\to\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}
is a parametrized
(guarded) monad morphism if for each
X\in|\mathbf{D}|
,

\alpha_{\operatorname{-\!-},X}\colon\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}X\to\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}X

is a (guarded) monad morphism.

A parametrized guarded monad
\mathbin{\hash}
is
guarded
(pre-)iterative
if each monad
\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}X
is guarded
(pre-)iterative and the monad morphisms
\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}f
are
iteration-preserving, i.e.

\displaystyle(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f)\,g^{\dagger}=((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}f)\,g)^{\dagger}.

(4)

Note that by Lemma
3.11
, condition (
4
)
is automatic for guarded iterative parametrized monads.

In the sequel, we tend to use the same notation for parametrized
monads as for the non-parametrized case, assuming that omitted
information is understood from the context. For example, the monad unit

\eta_{X,Y}\colon X\to X\mathbin{\hash}Y
is additionally parametrized by
Y
, and
both parameters will be occasionally omitted unless confusion
arises. Kleisli lifting assigns
f^{\star}\colon X\mathbin{\hash}Z\to Y\mathbin{\hash}Z
to

f\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash}Z
, and for fixed
Z
all monad laws can be used
for parametrized monads as stated for non-parametrized monads. The
connection between Kleisli lifting and the functor part of the monad
can now be restated as follows:

(f\mathbin{\hash}\mathsf{id}_{Z})=(\eta_{Y,Z}f)^{\star}
where
f\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash}Z
.

Example 4.2
.

For purposes of the present work, the most important example
(taken from
[
35
]
) is

\mathbin{\hash}=T(\operatorname{-\!-}+\Sigma\operatorname{-\!-})\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
where

\mathbb{T}
is a (non-parametrized) monad on
\mathbf{C}
and
\Sigma
is an
endofunctor on
\mathbf{C}
. Informally,
\mathbb{T}
captures a computational
effect, e.g. nondeterminism for
T
being a (bounded) powerset monad, and
\Sigma

captures a signature of actions, e.g.
\Sigma X=A\times X
, as in
Example
3.14
. Specifically, taking
A=1
we obtain

X\mathbin{\hash}Y=T(X+Y)
; in this case, we have only one guard, which can
be interpreted as a delay. The second argument of
\mathbin{\hash}
can thus
be thought of as designated for guarded recursion.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\mathbin{\hash}\colon\mathbf{C}\times(\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D})\to\mathbf{C}
be a parametrized monad,
with unit
\eta
and Kleisli lifting
(-)^{\star}
. Then

\displaystyle X\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Y=\nu\gamma.\,X\mathbin{\hash}(\gamma,Y)

defines a
parametrized monad
\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}
, whose unit and Kleisli lifting we denote

\eta^{\nu}
and
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\text{\kreuz}}
, respectively.
Moreover,

(1)

If
\mathbin{\hash}
is guarded, then so is
\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}
, with guardedness defined as follows: given

\sigma\colon Y^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\kern 3.0pt$\mathrel{\hbox to9.87pt{\vbox to2.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower 1.52222pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} { {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.57 5.96 L 0 5.96 L 0 2.38 L 10.34 2.38}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.47221pt}{1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.77 0 L -1.66 2.21 L 0 0 L -1.66 -2.21}{stroke:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}$\kern 3.0pt}}Y
,
f\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z
is

\sigma
-guarded if
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash}(Y\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z,Z)

is
\sigma
-guarded; the correspondence
\mathbin{\hash}\mapsto\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}
extends
to a functor between the respective categories of guarded parametrized monads.

(2)

If
\mathbin{\hash}
is guarded pre-iterative, with an iteration operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
,
then so is
\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}
, with the iteration
operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ddagger}
sending
f\colon X\to_{2}(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z
to
f^{\ddagger}\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z
as follows:

\displaystyle f^{\ddagger}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\Bigl([\eta_{Y},(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\colon(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z\to Y\mathbin{\hash}(((Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z),Z)\Bigr)\,\eta^{\nu}_{Y+X,Z}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\kern-25.83325pt

(3)

If
\mathbin{\hash}
is guarded iterative, then so
is
\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}
, with solutions described as in the previous
clause.

To better understand the typing in the second clause above, note
that

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f\colon X\to_{2}(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash}((Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z,Z)
, so

•

(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash}((Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z,Z)
;

•

the right-most occurrence of
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
has type

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\colon(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z\to(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash}((Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z,Z);

•

the
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\dots)
subterm has type
(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z\to Y\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z
.

In case there is only one parameter of type
\mathbf{C}
, i.e.
\mathbin{\hash}\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
, the typing simplifies slightly: Now

\hash^{\nu}
is just a monad on
\mathbf{C}
, which we denote by
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}

(i.e.
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}X=X\hash^{\nu}()
). We write
\eta^{\nu}
,

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\text{\kreuz}}
for the corresponding monad structure. Then
given
f\colon X\to_{2}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(Y+X)
,

•

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f\colon X\to_{2}(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(Y+X)
;

•

(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}\colon X\to_{2}Y\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(Y+X)
;

•

the right-most occurrence of
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
has type

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\colon\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(Y+X)\to(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(Y+X);

•

the
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\dots)
subterm has type
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(Y+X)\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y
;

•

and, of course,
f^{\ddagger}\colon X\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y
.

Proof 4.4

(Proof (Theorem
4.3
))
.

(
1
): By currying we equivalently view
\mathbin{\hash}
as a functor from

\mathbf{D}
to the category of parametrized guarded monads of type

\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
, and the transformation
\mathbin{\hash}\mapsto\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}

as given pointwise.
It therefore suffices to show that the
assignment

\mathbin{\hash}\mapsto\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}=\nu\gamma.\,X\mathbin{\hash}\gamma

extends to a functor
from parametrized guarded monads of type
\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
to
guarded monads over
\mathbf{C}
where guardedness for
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{}
is defined as follows:

f\colon X\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y
is
\sigma
-guarded iff

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y
is
\sigma
-guarded w.r.t.
\mathbin{\hash}
.
Uustalu
[
35
]
already proves
that
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}
is a monad; we proceed to check that his
construction is in fact functorial.

As indicated above, we denote the monad structure on
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}
by

\eta^{\nu}
,
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\text{\kreuz}}
. These data are uniquely
determined by commutation of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\hash\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\hash\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\nu}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}+\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\hash(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}+\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\hash(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[f^{\text{\kreuz}},\,\mathsf{id}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\hat{f},\,(Y\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\hash[f^{\text{\kreuz}},\,\mathsf{id}]}

(5)

where

\displaystyle\hat{f}=

\displaystyle\;\Bigl(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}X\hash\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}\xrightarrow{X\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}}X\hash(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}{+}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y})\xrightarrow{\;\;\bar{f}^{\star}}Y\hash(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}+\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\bar{f}=

\displaystyle\;\Bigl(X\xrightarrow{\;f}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}X\hash\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}\xrightarrow{Y\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}}Y\hash(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}+\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y})\Bigr).

That is,
\eta^{\nu}_{X}
is the unique
(X\mathbin{\hash}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra
morphism
(X,\eta_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}})\to(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X},\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})
, and
[f^{\text{\kreuz}},\mathsf{id}]
is
the unique
(Y\mathbin{\hash}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra morphism

(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}+\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y},\;[\hat{f},(Y\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}])\to(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y},\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}),

the latter being essentially a definition of
f^{\text{\kreuz}}
by primitive
corecursion. In the sequel, we will omit the object part of
coalgebras when convenient, saying, e.g., that
\eta^{\nu}_{X}
is a
coalgebra morphism
\eta_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
.

We need to define the action of
\digamma\kern-3.0pt
on morphisms: Let
\mathbin{\hash^{\prime}}
be a
further parametrized monad, with all data of
\hash^{\prime}
and

\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}
indicated by primes, and let

\alpha\colon\mathbin{\hash}\to\mathbin{\hash^{\prime}}

be a parametrized monad morphism. We then define a monad morphism

\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}\colon\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}
by commutation of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mathbin{\hash^{\prime}}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\hash^{\prime}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}{X},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\mathbin{\hash}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}}

i.e.
(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}
is the unique
(X\mathbin{\hash^{\prime}}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra
morphism
(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X},\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\to(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}{X},\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime})
.

We first check functoriality of
\digamma\kern-3.0pt
. For preservation of identities,
just note that
\mathsf{id}\colon(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X},\mathsf{id}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\to(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X},\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})
is a
coalgebra morphism. For preservation of composition, we have that if

\beta\colon\hash^{\prime}\to\hash^{\prime\prime}
is a further parametrized monad morphism
then by naturality of
\beta
, the
(X\mathbin{\hash^{\prime}}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra
morphism
(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}\colon\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}
is also an

(X\mathbin{\hash^{\prime\prime}}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra morphism

\beta\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\to\beta\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}
; so
(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\beta})_{X}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}
is a
coalgebra morphism
\beta\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime\prime}
, and hence equals

(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\beta\alpha})_{X}
.

It remains to verify that
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}
is indeed a monad morphism.
First, we show compatibility with the unit, i.e.

(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}\,\eta^{\nu}_{X}={\eta^{\prime}}^{\nu}_{X}\colon X\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}{X}.

We note that by naturality of
\alpha
, the

(X\mathbin{\hash}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra morphism

\eta^{\nu}_{X}\colon\eta_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
is also an

(X\mathbin{\hash^{\prime}}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra morphism

\eta^{\prime}_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}=\alpha\,\eta_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}\to\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
, so that

(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}\,\eta^{\nu}_{X}
is a coalgebra morphism

\eta^{\prime}_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}
and hence equals
{\eta^{\prime}}^{\nu}_{X}
.

For compatibility of
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}
with Kleisli lifting, we have to
show that for
f\colon X\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}
,

(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}\,f)^{\text{\kreuz}^{\prime}}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}=\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}\,f^{\text{\kreuz}}.

We strengthen this goal to one concerning
[f^{\text{\kreuz}},\mathsf{id}]
, specifically
we show that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}+\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}{X}+\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[f^{\text{\kreuz}}\!,\,\mathsf{id}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f)^{\text{\kreuz}^{\prime}}\!,\,\mathsf{id}]}

commutes. By definition, the bottom arrow is a

(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra morphism

[\widehat{(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f},(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}]\to\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime},

and by now-familiar arguments, the top and right-hand arrows compose
to yield a
(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra morphism

[\alpha\hat{f},\alpha(Y\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}]\to\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}.

It therefore suffices to show that

(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}\colon[\alpha\hat{f},\alpha(Y\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}]\to[\widehat{(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f},(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}]

is a
(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra morphism. We first check
commutation of the corresponding square on the right-hand summand

\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{Y}
:

\displaystyle(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))\alpha(Y\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/ naturality of
\alpha

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})(Y\hash^{\prime}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y})\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}.

/​​/ definition of
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}

For commutation on the left-hand summand we have to show that

(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))\,\alpha\hat{f}=\widehat{(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}.

(6)

We rewrite the left-hand side of (
6
):

\displaystyle(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))\,\alpha\hat{f}

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))\,\alpha\bar{f}^{\star}(X\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/ definition of
\hat{f}

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))\,(\alpha\bar{f})^{\star^{\prime}}\alpha(X\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/
\alpha
a monad morphism

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))\,(\alpha\bar{f})^{\star^{\prime}}(X\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}.

/​​/ naturality of
\alpha

We next rewrite the right-hand side of (

/​​/ definition of

\displaystyle=\overline{(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f}^{\star^{\prime}}(X\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})(X\hash^{\prime}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X})\,\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/ definition of

\displaystyle=\overline{(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f}^{\star^{\prime}}(X\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))(X\mathbin{\hash^{\prime}}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}})\,\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

It thus suffices to show that

(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))(\alpha\bar{f})^{\star^{\prime}}=\overline{(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f}^{\star^{\prime}}(X\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y})).

We further rewrite the right-hand side
of (

\displaystyle\overline{(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f}^{\star^{\prime}}(X\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))

\displaystyle=((Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\prime}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}f)^{\star^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad(X\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))

/​​/ definition of

/​​/ definition of

\displaystyle\qquad\quad(X\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}((\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{X}+(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})_{Y}))((Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\star^{\prime}}

where we use in the last step that

is a monad morphism. We

But this is straightforward:

\displaystyle\alpha\bar{f}

\displaystyle=\alpha\,(Y\hash\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f

/​​/ definition of
\bar{f}

\displaystyle=(Y\hash^{\prime}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f.

/​​/ naturality of
\alpha

Next, we need to check the axioms of guarded monads for

\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}
.

•

(trv)
Let
f\colon X\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f=(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\mathbin{\hash}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}))\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f.

By
(trv)
for
\mathbin{\hash}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded,
and thus, by definition so is
(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}})f
.

•

(cmp)
Let
f\colon X\to_{2}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(Y+Z)
,

g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}V
,
h\colon Z\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}V
. Then
we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,[g,h]^{\star}f=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,g,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,h]^{\star}\,(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[g,h]^{\star})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f.

By assumption
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded, and therefore, since
\mathbin{\hash}
is a
parametrized guarded monad, so is
(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[g,h]^{\star})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f
.
Also, by assumption,
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}h
is
\sigma
-guarded.
By
(cmp)
for
\mathbin{\hash}
, this implies that the composite

[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,g,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,h]^{\star}\,(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[g,h]^{\star})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f

is
\sigma
-guarded and thus so is
[g,h]^{\star}f
.

•

(par)
Let
f_{i}\colon X_{i}\to_{\sigma}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y
for

, which by definition means that

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f_{i}\colon X_{i}\to_{\sigma}Y\hash(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y)
. By
(par)

for
\hash
,

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[f_{1},f_{2}]=[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f_{2}]\colon X_{i}\to_{\sigma}Y\hash(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y)
,
so that
[f_{1},f_{2}]\colon X_{1}+X_{2}\to_{\sigma}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}Y
as required.

This shows that
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}
is indeed a guarded monad; it remains
to show that given a parametrized guarded monad morphism

\alpha\colon\hash\to\hash^{\prime}
as above, the monad morphism
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}

preserves guardedness. That is, for
f\colon Z\to_{\sigma}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}V
we
have to show that
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}f\colon Z\to_{\sigma}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash^{\prime}}V
, i.e. that
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}f)
is
\sigma
-guarded. Indeed, by definition
of
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha}\,f)=(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})\,\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f.

By assumption,
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f
is
\sigma
-guarded and therefore, since
\mathbin{\hash}

is a parametrized guarded monad and
\alpha
is a parametrized guarded
monad morphism, so is

(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\alpha})\,\alpha\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f
.

(
2
): Let
f\colon X\to_{2}(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z
, and let

g=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\mathbin{\hash}\mathsf{id})\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}\colon X\to_{2}(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z

. Indeed,

,

.

(8)

\displaystyle=[\eta,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f^{\ddagger}]^{\star}(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[\eta^{\nu},f^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f

/​​/ (

)

\displaystyle\;=[\eta,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},f^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\,g]^{\star}(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[\eta^{\nu},f^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f

/​​/ definition of

\displaystyle\qquad\quad(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[\eta^{\nu},f^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f

/​​/ (

/​​/ definition of

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f^{\ddagger}.

/​​/ definition of

Property (

(
3
): We have to show that, given

\displaystyle\;=((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[\eta^{\nu},f^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\;=[\eta,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f^{\ddagger}]^{\star}(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}[\eta^{\nu},f^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\mathbin{\hash}\mathsf{id})\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}

), definition of

\displaystyle\;=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f^{\ddagger},

/​​/ definition of
f^{\ddagger}

we obtain

using the fact that
