is an
isomorphism.

Remark 4.5
.

The definitions figuring in Theorem
4.3
specialize
to two generic cases occurring in previous literature:

(1)

With
\mathbf{D}=1
,
\mathbin{\hash}=T(\operatorname{-\!-}+\Sigma\operatorname{-\!-})
for an
endofunctor
\Sigma
and a totally guarded pre-iterative monad

\mathbb{T}=(T,\eta,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\star},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\dagger})
, we obtain the
setting studied by Goncharov et al.
[
18
]
:

\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{}
is isomorphically a monad
\mathbb{T}_{\Sigma}
on

\mathbf{C}
with
T_{\Sigma}X=\nu\gamma.\,T(X+\Sigma\gamma)
, unit

\eta^{\nu}=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}
, with Kleisli lifting

(f\colon X\to T_{\Sigma}Y)^{\text{\kreuz}}
uniquely determined by the
equation

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f^{\text{\kreuz}}=[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f,\operatorname{}\linebreak\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\Sigma f^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},

and with the total iteration operator

\displaystyle\bigl(f\colon X\to T_{\Sigma}(Y+X)\bigr)^{\ddagger}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(\bigl[[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},(T[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bigr]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}.

(2)

With
\mathbf{D}=1
, and any vacuously guarded
\mathbin{\hash}\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{C}
, we obtain the setting
of Uustalu
[
35
]
, with the guarded iterative monad
\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{}=\nu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}\gamma

defined as follows: The monad structure is specified by (
5
), and the
iteration operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ddagger}
is uniquely determined by the equation

[\eta^{\nu},f^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}=f^{\ddagger}
for every
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded
f\colon X\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{(Y+X)}
.
According to Theorem
4.3
(2),
f
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded
iff
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f\colon X\to(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{(Y+X)}
factors through
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\mathbin{\hash}\mathsf{id}\colon Y\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{(Y+X)}\to(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash}\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{(Y+X)}
,
which is precisely the notion of guardedness in
[
35
]
.

Example 4.6
.

We proceed to illustrate the use of Theorem
4.3
by various instances
of Example
3.14
.

(1)

By equipping the finite powerset monad
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}
on
\mathbf{Set}
with vacuous
guardedness, we obtain by Theorem
4.3
a notion of guardedness for
\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}(X+A\times\gamma)
,
which allows for systems consisting of equations of the form

\displaystyle x=y_{1}+\ldots+y_{n}+a_{1}.\,t_{1}+\ldots+a_{m}.\,t_{m}

(9)

where the variables
y_{i}
are not allowed to occur on the left-hand side
and the terms
t_{i}
represent elements of
\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}(X+A\times\gamma)
,
as previously explained in Example
3.14
. By Proposition
5
and
Theorem
4.3
, we conclude that these systems have unique
solutions, which is of course a known fact in process algebra. This and the
following examples are intentionally chosen to be simple for illustrative
purposes, but we emphasize that the same principles apply to the examples obtained
by replacing
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}
with a more general
T
and
A\times\operatorname{-\!-}
with a more general
\Sigma
.
For example, replacing
T
with a
subdistribution monad

[
20
]
,
to model probability instead on nondeterminism, would require changing the format
of (
9
) to

\displaystyle x=p_{1}\cdot y_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}\cdot y_{n}+p^{\prime}_{1}\cdot a_{1}.\,t_{1}+\ldots+p^{\prime}_{m}\cdot a_{m}.\,t_{m}

where the non-negative real coefficients
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n},p_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,p_{m}^{\prime}
, subject
to the condition
p_{1}+\ldots+p_{n}+p_{1}^{\prime}+\ldots+p_{m}^{\prime}\leq 1
, represent the probabilities
of choosing the corresponding alternative.

(2)

By replacing
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}
with countable powerset
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
in the
previous clause, we can relax the format of equation systems that
can be solved, at the price of losing uniqueness of
solutions. Specifically, let
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
be totally guarded
pre-iterative with solutions of
f\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(Y+X)
calculated via
least fixpoints. The derived notion of guardedness for

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
according to
Theorem
4.3
is again total, i.e. allows solving
arbitrary systems of equations (we discuss an application of such
unguarded recursive process definitions
in
[
18
, Section 3]
; specifically, they allow
defining countably branching systems in basic process algebra).
The canonical derived iteration operator makes use of both least
fixpoints and unique coalgebraic fixpoints. For example, the
canonical solution of

\displaystyle x=x+a.\,x

(10)

is
the infinite sequence
x=a^{\omega}
, seen as an element of the
final countably branching labelled transition system

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(A\times\gamma)
– intuitively, the original
system (
10
) is first collapsed to
x=a.\,x
, iterating away the first

x
in the sum by taking a least fixpoint, and the resulting system
is solved uniquely. In detail, the definitions in
Theorem
4.3
unfold as follows. We have the case
mentioned in Example
4.2
where

X\mathbin{\hash}Y={\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times Y)
(so

\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}=\nu\gamma.{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
). Our example equation (
10
)
corresponds to the map
f\colon X\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(\iobj+X)
where
X=\{x\}

and

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f(x)=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}x,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(\{x\})\rangle\}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}((\iobj+X)+A\times\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}(\iobj+X))\mathbin{\cong}{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(A\times\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}+X)
. The
definition of
(-)^{\ddagger}
according to Theorem
4.3

now tells us to first iterate
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f
in
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
, by taking a
least fixpoint with
x
seen as a variable, obtaining

(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(A\times\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X})
where

(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}(x)=\{\langle a,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\})\rangle\}.

We next form the map

g=({\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})[(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger},\eta]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\colon\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\iobj+A\times\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X})
,
where
\eta
and
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\star}
are the unit and the Kleisli lifting of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
, so for

t\in\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}=\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
,

\displaystyle g(t)

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}(x)\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle b,s\rangle\in A\times\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle b,s\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}

\displaystyle=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(\{x\}\rangle)\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle b,s\rangle\in A\times\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle b,s\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}.

We then obtain a final coalgebra morphism

\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g\colon\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}{X}\to\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}\iobj=\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(A\times\gamma)
.
The solution
f^{\ddagger}(x)
is obtained by applying
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g
to

\eta^{\nu}_{X}(x)=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(\eta_{X,\digamma\kern-3.0pt{}_{\hash}X}(x))=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\})
, using
the description of
\eta^{\nu}
recalled in the proof of
Theorem
4.3
. Since

g(\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\}))=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\})\rangle\}
, we obtain
that
f^{\ddagger}(x)
is
a^{\omega}
, as expected.

(3)

Consider a further variation of the same example obtained by
replacing
A
in the previous example by
1+A
, where the adjoined
element is supposed to capture the invisible action
\tau
in the
usual sense of process algebra
[
25
]
. Applying
Theorem
4.3
to
X\mathbin{\hash}Y={\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+(1+A)\times Y)
as
in the previous example, we would derive a notion of guardedness
that identifies as guarded any recursive call preceded by an action,
visible or not. We can refine this view by allowing only visible
actions as guards, which is in fact standard for
CCS
[
25
]
. To this end, consider the obvious isomorphism

\displaystyle\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+(1+A)\times\gamma)\cong\nu\gamma^{\prime}.\,\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+\gamma+A\times\gamma^{\prime}),

which involves two more parametrized monads:

{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{-\!-}+\operatorname{-\!-}+A\times\operatorname{-\!-})\colon\mathbf{Set}\times(\mathbf{Set}\times\mathbf{Set})\to\mathbf{Set}

and

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{-\!-}+\gamma+A\times\operatorname{-\!-})\colon\mathbf{Set}\times\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Set}
.
The latter parametrized monad is formed on top of the former. We equip

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{-\!-}+\gamma+A\times\operatorname{-\!-})
with the
vacuous notion of guardedness. By furthermore forming the fixpoint

\nu\gamma^{\prime}.\,\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+\gamma+A\times\gamma^{\prime})
, we obtain
precisely the notion of guardedness we aimed at for the isomorphic
monad
\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}
.

(4)

Consider
TX={\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,X+1+A\times\gamma)
, which can be understood as a semantic
domain for processes with results in
X
as before, but now modulo
finite trace
equivalence
instead of strong bisimilarity as the underlying equivalence relation: the elements of
TX
are sets of traces from

\mu\gamma.\,X+1+A\times\gamma\cong A^{\star}+A^{\star}\times X
consisting
of terminating traces (from
A^{\star}\times X
) and non-terminating traces (from
A^{\star}
).
In order to apply our theory to this example, we make use of Hasuo et al.’s results on

coalgebraic finite trace semantics

[
20
]
. Specifically,
we make use of the fact that due to presence of a canonical distributive law

X+1+A\times{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+1+A\times\operatorname{-\!-})

and a suitable order-enrichment of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
, the object

\mu\gamma.\,X+1+A\times\gamma
computed in
\mathbf{Set}

carries a final coalgebra
\nu\gamma.\,X+1+A\times\gamma
in the Kleisli category
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
. In this category we equip the parametrized monad

\mathbin{\hash}=\operatorname{-\!-}+1+A\times\operatorname{-\!-}
with the vacuous notion of
guardedness and thus derive the notion of guardedness for

\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}
, allowing exactly for recursive calls preceded by
actions from
A
. Again, by Proposition
5
and by
Theorem
4.3
(3), the obtained monad is guarded
iterative.

Note that the monad
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}+1+A\times\gamma)
is arguably too large, as it contains sets
of traces not realized by any process from

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
. This can easily be fixed by
cutting down to the submonad of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}+1+A\times\gamma)
consisting of
the
prefix-closed
sets of traces, i.e. such sets
S
that

st\in S
implies
s\in S
and
\langle st,x\rangle\in S
implies
s\in S
. It
is easy to see that this is a guarded pre-iterative submonad of

\mathbb{T}
, and therefore guarded iterative.

5.
Complete Elgot Monads and Iteration Congruences

Figure 2
.
Axioms of guarded iteration.

(a)

Dinaturality 1:

g

Figure 3
.
Derivable laws of iteration.

Besides the fixpoint identity we are interested in
natural guarded versions of the classical properties of the iteration
operator, which we refer to as the
iteration
laws

[
10
,
6
,
32
]
:

•

naturality:

g^{\star}f^{\dagger}=([(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\,f)^{\dagger}
for
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
,
g\colon Y\to TZ
;

•

codiagonal:

(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f)^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger\dagger}
for
f\colon X\to_{12,2}T((Y+X)+X)
;

•

uniformity:

f\,h=T(\mathsf{id}+h)\,g
implies

f^{\dagger}\,h=g^{\dagger}
for
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
,
g\colon Z\to_{2}T(Y+Z)
and

h\colon Z\to X
.

Remarkably, this list does not include the
well-known
dinaturality law
, as is turns out to be derivable
(cf.
[
18
,
14
]
). We prove this further below.
The above axioms are summarized in graphical form in Figure
2
,
and then become quite intuitive.
We indicate the scope of the
iteration operator by a shaded box and guardedness by bullets at the
outputs of a morphism. Blue boxes indicate morphisms of the base category
\mathbf{C}
,
to contrast orange boxes referring to Kleisli morphisms.

A guarded pre-iterative monad is called a
complete Elgot monad

if it is totally guarded and satisfies all iteration laws. In the
sequel we shorten ‘complete Elgot monads’ to ‘Elgot monads’ (to be
distinguished from Elgot monads in the sense
of
[
2
]
, which have solutions only for morphisms
with finitely presentable domain).

In general, the fact that the iteration laws are correctly formulated
relies on the axioms of guardedness.
For example, in
the codiagonal axiom, this follows
by
(cmp)
from the assumption

f\colon X\to_{12,2}T((Y+X)+X)
that
T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f
is

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded, and by Proposition
4
that

f^{\dagger}
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded. Indeed, the axioms for guarded monads
are designed precisely to enable the formulation of the iteration
laws.

We show next that for guarded
iterative
monads, all iteration
laws are automatic. In preparation, we prove the aforementioned fact that
dinaturality follows from the other axioms (thus generalizing corresponding recent
observations on iteration theories
[
18
,
14
]
).
Additionally, we show that the well-known Bekić identity is derivable
too.

Proposition 6
.

Any guarded pre-iterative monad satisfying naturality, codiagonal
and uniformity also satisfies

•

dinaturality:

([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}\,g)^{\dagger}=[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},g]^{\star}\,h)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\,g
for
g\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+Z)
and
h\colon Z\to T(Y+X)
or

g\colon X\to T(Y+Z)
and
h\colon Z\to_{2}T(Y+X)
;

•

Bekić identity:

\left(T[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,[f,g]\right)^{\dagger}=[h^{\dagger},[\eta,h^{\dagger}]^{\star}g^{\dagger}]
with
f\colon X\to_{12,2}T((Y+X)+Z)
,
g\colon Z\to_{12,2}T((Y+X)+Z)
, and
h=[\eta,g^{\dagger}]^{\star}f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
.

In axiomatizations of total iteration, the Bekić
identity is sometimes taken to replace codiagonal and
dinaturality
[
6
, Section 6.8]

[
2
,
18
]
. Both
dinaturality and the Bekić identity are again depicted graphically
in Figure
3
.
The two versions of the dinaturality axiom
correspond to the alternative sets of guardedness assumptions in its formulation;
basically, we need to distinguish cases on whether
the loop over
g
and
h
is guarded at
g
or at
h
.

Proof 5.1
.

Following
[
14
]
, we consider a specific instance of
uniformity:

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}=([T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f,h]:X+Z\to_{2}T(Y+(X+Z)))^{\dagger}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}

(11)

where
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
and
h\colon Z\to_{2}T(Y+(X+Z))
, and prove the following
instance of the Bekić identity:

\displaystyle(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[f,g]\colon X+Z\to T(Y+(X+Z)))^{\dagger}=[f^{\dagger},[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}g]

(12)

where
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
and
g\colon Z\to_{2}T(Y+X)
. Indeed, on the one hand, by (
11
),

\displaystyle\left(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[f,g]\right)^{\dagger}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}=f^{\dagger},

(13)

and on the other hand

\displaystyle(T(\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[f,g])^{\dagger}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\left(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[f,g]\right)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\,T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})[f,g]\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

/​​/ fixpoint

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\left(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[f,g]\right)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\,T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\left(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[f,g]\right)^{\dagger}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}]^{\star}\,g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}\,g.

/​​/ (
13
)

As the result we obtain (
12
). Analogously, we prove another
instance of the Bekić identity, namely

\displaystyle(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,[f,g]\colon X+Z\to T(Y+(X+Z)))^{\dagger}=[[\eta,g^{\dagger}]^{\star}f,g^{\dagger}]

(14)

with
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+Z)
and
g\colon Z\to_{2}T(Y+Z)
. We proceed to show that
under the other axioms, these two instances imply the full Bekić identity

\displaystyle(T[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,[f,g]\colon X+Z\to T(Y+(X+Z)))^{\dagger}=[h^{\dagger},[\eta,h^{\dagger}]^{\star}g^{\dagger}]

(15)

where
f\colon X\to_{12,2}T((Y+X)+Z)
and
g\colon Z\to_{12,2}T((Y+X)+Z)
and

h=[\eta,g^{\dagger}]^{\star}f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
. Let us argue briefly that
h
is

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded. Note that the assumption for
f
by
(iso)
implies that
f^{\prime}=T[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f\colon X\to T(Y+(X+Z))

is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded and therefore
h=[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},g^{\dagger}]]^{\star}f^{\prime}

is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded by
(cmp)
.

Now, the proof of (
15
) runs as follows:

\displaystyle(T[\mathsf{id}+

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,[f,g])^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,[f,g]\bigr)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,[f,g])^{\dagger\dagger}

/​​/ codiagonal

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,[T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\mathsf{id})\,f,T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\mathsf{id})\,g])^{\dagger\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta,(T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\mathsf{id})\,g)^{\dagger}]^{\star}T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\mathsf{id})\,f,

\displaystyle\qquad(T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\mathsf{id})\,g)^{\dagger}]^{\dagger}

/​​/ (
14
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}),(T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\mathsf{id})\,g)^{\dagger}]^{\star}f,

\displaystyle\qquad(T((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})+\mathsf{id})\,g)^{\dagger}]^{\dagger}

/​​/ naturality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[\eta,g^{\dagger}]^{\star}\,f,T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g^{\dagger}]^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[h,g^{\dagger}])^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[h^{\dagger},[\eta,h^{\dagger}]^{\star}g^{\dagger}].

/​​/ (
12
)

Finally, let us derive dinaturality from (
15
). Suppose that

g\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+Z)
and
h\colon Z\to T(Y+X)
satisfy either guardedness premise of the
dinaturality axiom and consider the following instance of (
15
) with

f
replaced by
T(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})\,g
and
g
replaced by
(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,h

(note that by the fixpoint identity,
((T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,h)^{\dagger}=h
):

\displaystyle[T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,g,T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,h]^{\dagger}=[([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}g)^{\dagger},[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}g)^{\dagger}]^{\star}h].

(16)

Let
\gamma_{X,Y}\colon Y+X\to Y+X
be the obvious symmetry transformation and note the
following simple consequence of uniformity:

\displaystyle[T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,g,T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,h]^{\dagger}\gamma=[T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,h,T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g]^{\dagger}.

(17)

By combining (
17
), (
16
) and the symmetric form of
the latter (with
h
and
g
switched), we obtain:

\displaystyle[([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}g)^{\dagger},[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}g)^{\dagger}]^{\star}h]=[[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},g]^{\star}h)^{\dagger}]^{\star}g,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},g]^{\star}h)^{\dagger}].

Dinaturality is now obtained by composing both sides with

\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\colon X\to X+Z
.

The proof of the following result runs in accordance with
the original ideas of Elgot for iterative theories
[
10
]
,
except that, by Proposition
6
, dinaturality is now
replaced with uniformity.

Theorem 5.2
.

Every guarded iterative monad validates naturality, dinaturality, codiagonal, and uniformity.

Proof 5.3
.

By Proposition
6
we only need to verify naturality, codiagonal and uniformity.

•

Naturality.
Let
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
and
g\colon Y\to TZ
. Then

\displaystyle g^{\star}f^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle\;g^{\star}[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[g^{\star}\eta,g^{\star}f^{\dagger}]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[g,g^{\star}f^{\dagger}]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,g^{\star}f^{\dagger}]^{\star}[(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}f.

Since the same equation uniquely characterizes
([(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\,f)^{\dagger}
,
the latter is equal to
g^{\star}f^{\dagger}
.

•

Codiagonal.
Let
f\colon X\to_{12,2}T((Y+X)+X)
. Then

\displaystyle f^{\dagger\dagger}=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,f^{\dagger\dagger}]^{\star}f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,f^{\dagger\dagger}]^{\star}[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta,f^{\dagger\dagger}],[\eta,f^{\dagger\dagger}]^{\star}f^{\dagger}]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\eta,f^{\dagger\dagger}],f^{\dagger\dagger}]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,f^{\dagger\dagger}]^{\star}T[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2},\mathsf{id}]\,f.

Therefore
f^{\dagger\dagger}
satisfies the fixpoint identity for
(T[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2},\mathsf{id}]\,f)^{\dagger}
,
and thus
f^{\dagger\dagger}=(T[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2},\mathsf{id}]\,f)^{\dagger}
.

•

Uniformity.
Suppose that
f\,h=T(\mathsf{id}+h)\,g
for some

f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
,
g\colon Z\to_{2}T(Y+Z)
and
h\colon Z\to X
. Then

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}h=[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}f\,h=[\eta,f^{\dagger}]^{\star}T(\mathsf{id}+h)\,g=[\eta,f^{\dagger}h]^{\star}g,

that is,
f^{\dagger}h
satisfies the fixpoint equation for
g^{\dagger}
. Hence
g^{\dagger}=f^{\dagger}h
.∎

We now proceed to introduce key properties of morphisms of
guarded monads that allow for transferring pre-iterativity and
the iteration laws, respectively.

Definition 5.4

(Guarded retraction)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbb{S}
be guarded monads. We call a monad morphism

\rho\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
a
guarded retraction
if there is a family of morphisms

(\upsilon_{X}\colon SX\to TX)_{X\in|\mathbf{C}|}
(not necessarily natural in
X
!)
such that

(1)

for every
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}SY
, we have
\upsilon_{Y}f\colon X\to_{\sigma}TY
, and

(2)

\rho_{X}\upsilon_{X}=\mathsf{id}
for all
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\rho\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
be a guarded retraction, witnessed by

\upsilon\colon\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{T}
, and suppose that
(\mathbb{T},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\dagger})

is guarded pre-iterative. Then
\mathbb{S}
is guarded pre-iterative with
the iteration operator
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ddagger}
given by

f^{\ddagger}=\rho\,(\upsilon f)^{\dagger}
.

Proof 5.6
.

Since
\mathbb{T}
satisfies the fixpoint identity,
[\eta,(\upsilon f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\upsilon f=(\upsilon f)^{\dagger}
and therefore,

\displaystyle f^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon f)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,[\eta,(\upsilon f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\upsilon f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\rho\eta,\rho(\upsilon f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\rho\upsilon f

/​​/
\rho
is a monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,f^{\ddagger}]^{\star}f.

/​​/
\rho\upsilon=\mathsf{id}

Definition 5.7

(Iteration congruence)
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be a guarded pre-iterative monad and let
\mathbb{S}
be a monad. We call a
monad morphism
\rho\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
an
iteration congruence
if for every pair
of morphisms
f,g\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
,

\displaystyle\rho f=\rho g\text{\hskip 20.44434ptimplies\hskip 20.44434pt}\rho f^{\dagger}=\rho g^{\dagger}.

(18)

If
\rho
is moreover a guarded retraction, we call
\rho
an

iteration-congruent retraction
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Under the premises of Theorem
5.5
, assume
moreover that
\rho
is an iteration-congruent retraction. Then any
property out of naturality, dinaturality, codiagonal, and uniformity
that is satisfied by
\mathbb{T}
is also satisfied by
\mathbb{S}
.

Proof 5.9
.

The crucial observation is that under our
assumptions, (
18
) is equivalent to the condition that
for all
f\colon X\to_{2}T(Y+X)
,

\displaystyle\rho\,(\upsilon\rho\,f)^{\dagger}=\rho f^{\dagger}.

(19)

Indeed, (
18
)
\implies
(
19
),
for
\rho\upsilon\rho\,f=\rho\,f
and therefore

\rho(\upsilon\rho\,f)^{\dagger}=\rho\,f^{\dagger}
and
conversely, assuming (
19
) both for
f
and
for
g
, and
\rho f=\rho g
, we obtain that

\rho f^{\dagger}=\rho(\upsilon\rho\,f)^{\dagger}=\rho(\upsilon\rho\,g)^{\dagger}=\rho g^{\dagger}
.
The proof of transfer of the respective properties then proceeds as follows.

•

Naturality:

\displaystyle g^{\star}f^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;g^{\star}\rho\,(\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\rho\upsilon\,g)^{\star}\,\rho\,(\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/
\rho\upsilon=\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon\,g)^{\star}\,(\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/
\rho
is a monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,([(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\upsilon\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/ naturality for
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon\rho\,[(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\upsilon\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/ (
19
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon[\rho\,(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\upsilon\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\rho\,\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/
\rho
is a monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon[(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/
\rho\upsilon=\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}f)^{\ddagger}.

•

Dinaturality:
First observe that it follows from the fact that
\rho

is a monad morphism and
\rho\upsilon=\mathsf{id}
that

\rho\upsilon[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}\,g=\rho[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\upsilon h]^{\star}\upsilon g
, and therefore, by (
18
),
that

\displaystyle\rho(\upsilon[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}\,g)^{\dagger}=\rho([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\upsilon h]^{\star}\upsilon g)^{\dagger}.

(20)

Then we obtain the goal as follows:

\displaystyle([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}\,g)^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;\rho(\upsilon[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},h]^{\star}\,g)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\upsilon h]^{\star}\upsilon g)^{\dagger}

/​​/ (
20
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\upsilon g]^{\star}\,\upsilon h)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\,\upsilon g

/​​/ dinaturality for
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\upsilon g]^{\star}\,\upsilon h)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\rho\upsilon g

/​​/
\rho
is a monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\upsilon g]^{\star}\,\upsilon h)^{\dagger}]^{\star}g

/​​/ since
\rho\upsilon=\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho(\upsilon[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},g]^{\star}h)^{\dagger}]^{\star}g

/​​/ analogous to (
20
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},g]^{\star}h)^{\ddagger}]^{\star}g.

•

Codiagonal:

\displaystyle(f^{\ddagger})^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon\rho(\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,((\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger})^{\dagger}

/​​/ (
19
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/ codiagonal for
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon\rho\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/ (
19
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\upsilon\,S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,\,f)^{\dagger}

/​​/
\rho\upsilon=\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f)^{\ddagger}.

•

Uniformity:
Suppose that
f\,h=S(\mathsf{id}+h)\,g
. Then

\displaystyle\upsilon\,fh=

\displaystyle\;\upsilon\,S(\mathsf{id}+h)\,g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\upsilon\,S(\mathsf{id}+h)\,\rho\upsilon\,g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;T(\mathsf{id}+h)\,\upsilon\,g

and therefore
(\upsilon\,f)^{\dagger}\,h=(\upsilon\,g)^{\dagger}
. This implies
f^{\ddagger}\,h=g^{\ddagger}
by definition.∎

Recall from the introduction that a monad
\mathbb{S}
is

iteratable
if its coinductive resumption transform

\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
exists. We make
\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
into a guarded monad by
applying Theorem
4.3
to
\mathbb{S}
as a vacuously guarded
monad; explicitly:
f\colon X\to S^{\nu}(Y+X)
is guarded iff

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f=S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})\,g\qquad\text{for some\qquad$g\colon X\to S(Y+S^{\nu}(Y+X))$}.

We are now set to prove our first main result, which states that every
iteratable Elgot monad can be obtained by quotienting a guarded
iterative monad; that is, every choice of solutions that obeys the
iteration laws arises by quotienting a more fine-grained model in
which solutions are uniquely determined:

Theorem 5.10
.

A totally guarded iteratable monad
\mathbb{S}
is an Elgot monad iff there
is a guarded iterative monad
\mathbb{T}
and an iteration-congruent
retraction
\rho\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
. Specifically, every iteratable Elgot
monad
\mathbb{S}
is an iteration-congruent retract of its coinductive
resumption transform
\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
.

Proof 5.11
.

‘If’ is immediate by Theorems
5.8

and
5.2
. We prove ‘only if’, i.e. that

\mathbb{S}=(S,\eta,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\star},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\dagger})
is an
iteration-congruent retract of

\mathbb{S}^{\nu}=(\nu\gamma.\,S(\operatorname{-\!-}+\gamma),\eta^{\nu},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\star},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\ddagger})
. We
define
\upsilon_{X}=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})
and

\rho_{X}=\bigl(S^{\nu}X\xrightarrow{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}}S(X+S^{\nu}X)\bigr)^{\dagger}.

Clearly,
\upsilon f
is
\sigma
-guarded for every
f\colon X\to SY
and
\upsilon
is left inverse to
\rho
, for

\displaystyle\rho\upsilon=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})

/​​/ fixpoint for
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})

/​​/ fixpoint for
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\eta^{\star}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathsf{id}.

It follows straightforwardly by naturality of
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
that
\rho

is a natural transformation. Note the following property of
\rho
: for any
h\colon X\to S(Y+X)
,

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)=S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)\,h
, and hence, by uniformity

\displaystyle\rho(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)=h^{\dagger}.

(21)

Let us verify that
\rho
is a monad morphism. For one thing

\displaystyle\rho\eta^{\nu}=[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\eta^{\nu}=[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}=\eta.

Next, we have to check that
\rho f^{\text{\kreuz}}=(\rho f)^{\star}\rho
for any
f\colon X\to S^{\nu}Y
.
Note that

\displaystyle f^{\text{\kreuz}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl([[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\,(S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}).

Therefore

\displaystyle\rho f^{\text{\kreuz}}=

\displaystyle\;\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl([[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\,(S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl([[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)^{\dagger}\,(S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})

/​​/ (
21
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,[(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)^{\dagger}\,(S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(([(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\dagger}\bigr)^{\dagger}\,(S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})

/​​/ codiagonal

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\,(S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})

/​​/ naturality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f]^{\star}\rho)^{\dagger}\,(S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\rho\,(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})

/​​/ dinaturality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\,\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}S(\mathsf{id}+S^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger})^{\star}\,\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(((S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger})^{\star}\,\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(((S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\rho f)^{\dagger})^{\star}\,\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta,((S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\rho f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,\rho f)^{\star}\,\rho

/​​/ fixpoint

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\rho f)^{\star}\rho.

Finally, let us check that
\rho
is an iteration
congruence. Let
f,g\colon X\to_{2}S^{\nu}(Y+X)
, which means that there are
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\colon X\to S(Y+S^{\nu}(X+Y))

such that
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f=S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})f^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f=S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})g^{\prime}
. Suppose that
\rho f=\rho g
,
which amounts to

\displaystyle[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}f^{\prime}=[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}g^{\prime},

(22)

for

\displaystyle\rho f=[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f=[\eta,\rho]^{\star}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})f^{\prime}=[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}f^{\prime}

and analogously for
g
. Our goal is to prove that

\displaystyle\rho f^{\ddagger}=[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}f^{\prime})^{\ddagger}]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2},

from which
\rho f^{\dagger}=\rho g^{\dagger}
will follow by the analogous formula for
\rho g^{\dagger}
and (
22
).
Observe that

\displaystyle f^{\ddagger}=(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)\,\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

where
h=[[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},f^{\prime}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
. Now

\displaystyle\rho f^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;h^{\dagger}\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

/​​/ (
21
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},f^{\prime}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\dagger}\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,[[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f^{\prime}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\dagger}\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(([[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f^{\prime}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

/​​/ codiagonal

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(([(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},f^{\prime}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},f^{\prime}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

/​​/ naturality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},f^{\prime}]^{\star}\rho)^{\dagger}\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}f^{\prime})^{\dagger}]^{\star}\rho\,\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

/​​/ dinaturality

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,([\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho]^{\star}f^{\prime})^{\dagger}]^{\star}\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

and we are done.

Example 5.12

(Finite trace semantics)
.

Let us revisit Example
4.6
(4), with
A
assumed to be finite
throughout. Recall that

\mu\gamma.\,(X+1)+A\times\gamma\cong A^{\star}+A^{\star}\times X
is a final
((X+1)+A\times\operatorname{-\!-})
-coalgebra in
the Kleisli category
\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}}
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
. Note that
\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)

is a coalgebra of the same type in the same category, with

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)\,\xrightarrow{~{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\cup\,\{\cdot\}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{!}~}\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}((X+1)+A\times\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma))

as the structure morphism, where
\cup
denotes pointwise union and

\{\cdot\}
is the map
x\mapsto\{x\}
, i.e. the unit of

{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
. Intuitively, we thus add ‘non-termination’, i.e. the element of
the right-hand summand
1
in
X+1
, as a possible result to every
state (in the original view of Hasuo et al.
[
20
]
,
this element instead represents acceptance, so the above definition
would correspond to converting a labelled transition system into an
automaton by making every state accepting). This yields a final
coalgebra map

\xi_{X}\colon\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,{(X+1)}+A\times\gamma)
characterized by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X+1)+A\times\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu\gamma.\,(X+1)+A\times\gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X+1)+A\times\mu\gamma.\,(X+1)+A\times\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\cup\,\{\cdot\}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{!}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X+1+A\times\xi_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\text{in}~\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}

which amounts to the following corecursive definition of
\xi_{X}
:

\xi_{X}(t)=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi_{X}(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}.

The result of applying
\xi_{X}
to a tree is the set of finite traces in it, which
are finite sequences from
A^{\star}
followed either by an element of
X

(successfully terminating traces) or by the single inhabitant of
1

(divergent traces).
It is easy to see that
\xi
is a natural transformation; we show that
it is in fact a monad morphism. The domain of
\xi
is a generalized
coalgebraic resumption monad

{\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{-\!-}+A\times\gamma)}
(on
\mathbf{Set}
) as
discussed in Example
3.14
, while the codomain

{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,(\operatorname{-\!-}+1)+A\times\gamma)
is obtained by
sandwiching the monad
\nu\gamma.\,(\operatorname{-\!-}+1)+A\times\gamma
(on

\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}}
) between the adjoint pair

F\dashv G\colon\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}}\to\mathbf{Set}
generating the monad
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
, and
therefore is also a monad (cf. Section
2
). The
corresponding structure is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\eta(x)=

\displaystyle\;\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\}

\displaystyle\bigl(f\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,(Y+1)+A\times\gamma)\bigr)^{\star}(p)=

\displaystyle\;\bigcup~\{(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\hat{f})(t)\mid t\in p\}

where
\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\hat{f}\colon\mu\gamma.\,(X+1)+A\times\gamma\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,(Y+1)+A\times\gamma)
is the initial
algebra morphism to the algebra
({\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,(Y+1)+A\times\gamma),\hat{f})
whose structure map

\hat{f}\colon(X+1)+A\times{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,(Y+1)+A\times\gamma)\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,(Y+1)+A\times\gamma)

is as follows:
\hat{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x)=f(x)
,
\hat{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star)=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}
,

\hat{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t\rangle)=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t\rangle\}
.
This results in the following inductive definition of
\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\hat{f}
:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\hat{f})\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x=

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\hat{f})\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star=

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\hat{f})\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t\rangle=

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime}\in(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\hat{f})(t)\}

It is then easy to see that
\xi
respects

\eta
. The fact that
\xi
respects Kleisli lifting amounts to a rather technical
verification of the fact that both
\xi f^{\star}
and
(\xi f)^{\star}\xi
satisfy
the same corecursive definition and are thus equal:

\displaystyle\xi\,f^{\star}(p)=\;

\displaystyle\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f^{\star}(p)\bigr\}\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f^{\star}(p)\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f(x)\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\bigr\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,f^{\star}(p^{\prime})\rangle\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,p^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f(x)\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,f^{\star}(p^{\prime})\rangle\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,p^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f(x),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f(x),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(f^{\star}(p^{\prime})),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,p^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}

\displaystyle(\xi f)^{\star}(\xi(p))=\;

\displaystyle\bigcup\,\{(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\widehat{\xi\,f})(t)\mid t\in\xi(p)\}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\bigcup\,\bigl\{(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\widehat{\xi\,f})(t)\mid t\in\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigcup\,\bigl\{(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\widehat{\xi\,f})(t)\mid t\in\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\bigcup\,\bigl\{(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\widehat{\xi\,f})(t)\mid t\in\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\bigcup\,\{\xi f(x)\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t\rangle\mid t\in(\operatorname{\mathsf{iter}}\widehat{\xi\,f})(t^{\prime\prime}),t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f(x),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in\xi(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f(x),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}

\displaystyle\quad\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\mid t^{\prime\prime}\in(\xi f)^{\star}(\xi(t^{\prime})),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}p\}

Now consider the situation where guardedness for
\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)

is induced by vacuous guardedness for
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{-\!-}+A\times\gamma)
by Theorem
4.3
(1)
and with guardedness for
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,{(X+1)}+A\times\gamma)
defined as follows:

f\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,(Y+1)+A\times\gamma)
is
\sigma
-guarded iff as a
morphism
f\colon X\to\nu\gamma.\,(Y+1)+A\times\gamma
in
\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}}
it is

\sigma
-guarded under the notion of guardedness induced by vacuous guardedness
for
(\operatorname{-\!-}+1)+A\times\operatorname{-\!-}
in
\mathbf{Set}_{{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}}
, again by Theorem
4.3
(1).
This turns
\xi
into a guarded monad morphism, and moreover
\xi
is iteration-preserving by
Lemma
3.11
, because, as we argued before in Example
4.6
(4),
its codomain
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,{(X+1)}+A\times\gamma)
is guarded iterative (a more abstract argument showing that sandwiching a guarded
iterative monad between an adjoint pair produces a guarded iterative monad is later given in Theorem
6.1
).

In order to obtain a guarded retraction from
\xi
, let
\rho
be the
epimorphic part of the image factorization of
\xi
. It is easy to verify
that the codomain of
\rho
consists precisely of the prefix-closed
subsets of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,{(X+1)}+A\times\gamma)
, i.e. is the
guarded iterative submonad of

{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,{(X+1)}+A\times\gamma)
mentioned in
Example
4.6
(4). Under the
axiom of choice
,
this is sufficient to turn
\rho
into a retraction because every epi
splits. However, the requisite section
\upsilon
can also be
constructed explicitly without choice, for every prefix-closed subset
of
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\mu\gamma.\,{(X+1)}+A\times\gamma)
standardly induces an

A
-branching tree, hence an element of

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
. In summary,

\displaystyle\rho_{X}(t)=

\displaystyle\;\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\!\mid\!\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\star\}\cup\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime\prime}\rangle\!\mid\!t^{\prime\prime}\in\rho_{X}(t^{\prime}),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t^{\prime}\rangle\in\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t\}

\displaystyle\upsilon_{X}(S)=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\bigl(\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}x\in S\bigr\}\cup\bigl\{\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,\upsilon_{X}(\{t\mid\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\langle a,t\rangle\in S\})\rangle\mid a\in A\bigr\}\bigr)

where
t\in\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
and
S
is a countable
prefix-closed subset of
\mu\gamma.\,{(X+1)}+{A\times\gamma}
. Note
that the tree constructed by
\upsilon
has only very special kind of
nondeterminism, not including non-deterministic choice between
processes prefixed by actions. Roughly, we can have
x+y
and
x+a.t

in the image of
\upsilon
with
x,y\in X
and
a\in A
, but not

a.t+b.s
with
a,b\in A
. The composition
\upsilon\rho
can
therefore be seen as a determinization procedure, pushing the
non-deterministic choice downwards along the tree. Of course,
non-determinism can not be entirely eliminated, because in the end we
arrive at subsets of
X
, which must remain intact. We conjecture that
this effect is generic, i.e. that the same scenario can be run with

\omega_{1}
replaced by any other regular infinite cardinal
\kappa
;
that is,
\upsilon\rho
pushes
\kappa
-branching non-determinism
downwards in the same sense as above. We also conjecture that

\upsilon
is a monad morphism and hence so is
\upsilon\rho
.

The established retraction
(\rho,\upsilon)
can thus be reused in two further cases.

Guarded iteration for finitely-branching
processes
We can restrict
\rho
to the monad

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega}(\operatorname{-\!-}+A\times X)
capturing finitely branching
processes with outputs in
X
. As indicated above, we then essentially
again obtain countable prefix-closed sets
P
of traces as the image
of
\rho
, which however now additionally satisfy the condition that
for each
w\in A^{*}
, the set
\{x\in X\mid(w,x)\in P\}
is finite
(while in the countably branching case, and for infinite
X
, these
sets may be countably infinite). The section
\upsilon
restricts
accordingly, and we thus obtain a guarded retraction.

Unguarded iteration for countably-branching processes
As discussed in Example
4.6
(2),

\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
supports
unguarded iteration, and in fact is an Elgot monad
[
18
]
.
In the remainder of the example we use the terms “unguarded” for total guardedness
and “guarded” for the notion of guardedness on
\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(X+A\times\gamma)
discussed above.
Now, in order to conclude by Theorem
5.10
that the codomain
of
\rho
as above is an Elgot monad, it suffices to check that
\rho
remains iteration preserving if we equip its domain with total guardedness, i.e. that
\rho
preserves iteration also of unguarded morphisms.
So let
f\colon X\to\nu\gamma.\,{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}((Y+X)+A\times\gamma)
. The unguarded iterate

f^{\ddagger}
is defined as the guarded iterate
\hat{f}^{\ddagger}
, where
\hat{f}
has
the same profile as
f
and is defined as the guarded morphism

\hat{f}=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})\,\bigl({\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f\bigr)^{\dagger},

with iteration
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
on
{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}
calculated in the
expected way using least fixpoints
[
18
]
. It is easy
to check that

\upsilon\rho\hat{f}=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}{\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id})\,\bigl({\mathcal{P}}_{\omega_{1}}[\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\upsilon\rho f\bigr)^{\dagger}

and thus
(\upsilon\rho\hat{f})^{\ddagger}=(\upsilon\rho f)^{\ddagger}
by
the above definition of
(\upsilon\rho f)^{\ddagger}
. Therefore,
using (
19
) and the fact that, as we argued
above,
\rho
preserves guarded iteration,

\rho f^{\ddagger}=\rho\hat{f}^{\ddagger}=\rho(\upsilon\rho\hat{f})^{\ddagger}=\rho(\upsilon\rho f)^{\ddagger}
,
which means that
\rho
is iteration preserving.

Recall from Section
4
that guardedness,
guarded iteration, and the coinductive resumption transform work at
the level of
parametrized monads
, i.e. functors from a
parameter category
\mathbf{D}
into the category of monads on a
category
\mathbf{C}
, typically rearranged into bifunctors

\hash\colon\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}
. The notions of guarded retraction and
iteration congruence extend straightforwardly to parametrized monads;
explicitly:

Definition 5.13
.

A parametrized guarded monad morphism is a
guarded retraction

(an
iteration congruence
) if its components are guarded
retractions (iteration congruences).

We then can take the claims of Theorem
4.3

further:

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
\mathbin{\hash},\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\colon\mathbf{C}\times(\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{D})\to\mathbf{C}
be
guarded parametrized monads, and let

\rho\colon\mathbin{\hash}\to\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}
be an iteration-congruent
retraction. By Theorem
4.3
,

\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}=\nu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}(\gamma,\operatorname{-\!-})
and

\mathbin{\hat{\hash}^{\nu}}=\nu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}(\gamma,\operatorname{-\!-})

are also parametrized guarded monads. Then

\rho^{\nu}\colon\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}\to\mathbin{\hat{\hash}^{\nu}}
,
with components

\displaystyle\rho^{\nu}_{X,Y}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(\nu\gamma.\,X\mathbin{\hash}(\gamma,Y)\xrightarrow{~\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}~}X\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}(\nu\gamma.\,X\mathbin{\hash}(\gamma,Y),Y)\bigr),

is again an iteration-congruent retraction.

Proof 5.15
.

It is already shown in Theorem
4.3
that
\rho^{\nu}
is a monad morphism.

We define the associated section by
\upsilon^{\nu}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\upsilon\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})
. Indeed it is easy to check
that
\rho^{\nu}\upsilon^{\nu}=\mathsf{id}
: since

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,\rho^{\nu}\upsilon^{\nu}=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\rho^{\nu})\,\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\upsilon^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\rho^{\nu})\,\rho(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\upsilon^{\nu})\,\upsilon\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\rho^{\nu})(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\upsilon^{\nu})\,\rho\upsilon\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\rho^{\nu}\upsilon^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}.

and also
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\mathsf{id}=(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\mathsf{id})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
, the claim
\rho^{\nu}\upsilon^{\nu}=\mathsf{id}
follows by uniqueness of final coalgebra morphisms.

Next, suppose that
f\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hat{\hash}^{\nu}}Z
is

\sigma
-guarded, which according to
Theorem
4.3
means that
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f
is

\sigma
-guarded. We need to show that so is

\upsilon^{\nu}f\colon X\to Y\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z
. Now

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\upsilon^{\nu}f=(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\upsilon^{\nu})\,\upsilon\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f

is
\sigma
-guarded because
\rho
is a guarded retraction and hence

\upsilon\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f
is
\sigma
-guarded, and
\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\upsilon^{\nu}
is
a parametrized guarded monad morphism. Hence, again, according to
Theorem
4.3
,
\upsilon^{\nu}f
is
\sigma
-guarded. We
have thus proved that
\rho^{\nu}
is a guarded retraction.

We are left to check that
\rho^{\nu}
is an iteration congruence. Suppose that

\rho^{\nu}f=\rho^{\nu}g
for some
f,g\colon X\to_{2}(Y+X)\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}Z
. Then

\displaystyle\rho\,(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f=(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\rho^{\nu})\,\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\rho^{\nu}f

(by naturality of
\rho
and the definition of
\rho^{\nu}
) and
analogously for
g
in place of
f
, so using that
\rho
is an
iteration congruence, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}=\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}g)^{\dagger}.

(23)

Observe that
for suitably typed
h
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,\rho\,(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h))\,h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h))\,\rho\,h,

and therefore, by finality of
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho h)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}h)=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho h).

(24)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\rho^{\nu}f^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;\rho^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

/​​/ Theorem
4.3

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho[\eta,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}

/​​/ (
24
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,\rho^{\nu}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}.

The last step is due to uniqueness of the final coalgebra morphism
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})
and
the following calculation:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(

\displaystyle[\eta,\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\,\rho^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\;\quad[\eta,\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\rho^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\;\quad\rho\,[\eta,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/
\rho
is a monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)

\displaystyle\;\quad\rho\,(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,[\eta,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/
\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu}
is a monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bigl(\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([\eta,\rho\,((\mathsf{id}\mathbin{\hash}\rho^{\nu})\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,\rho^{\nu}\big)

\displaystyle\;\quad\rho\,[\eta,(\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}f)^{\dagger}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}.

An analogous calculation applies to
\rho^{\nu}g^{\ddagger}
, and therefore
by (
23
),
\rho^{\nu}f^{\ddagger}=\rho^{\nu}g^{\ddagger}
.

Theorems
5.10
and
5.14

jointly provide a simple and structured way of showing that Elgotness
extends along the parametrized monad transformer

\hash{}{}\mapsto\mathbin{\hat{\hash}}
: If
\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}X
is Elgot,
then by Theorem
5.10
there is an
iteration-congruent retraction

\rho\colon\nu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}+\gamma\mathbin{\hash}X\to\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}X
. By
Theorem
5.14
, this gives rise to an iteration-congruent
retraction

\rho^{\nu}\colon\nu\gamma^{\prime}.\,\nu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}+\gamma\mathbin{\hash}(\gamma^{\prime},X)\to\nu\gamma^{\prime}.\,\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}(\gamma^{\prime},X)

and by Theorem
5.10
, the right-hand side is
again Elgot. We have thus proved

Corollary 7
.

Given a parametrized monad
\mathbin{\hash}
and
X\in|\mathbf{C}|
, if
\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}X
is Elgot then so is
\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash^{\nu}}X=\nu\gamma.\,\operatorname{-\!-}\mathbin{\hash}(\gamma,X)
.

In particular, we have thus obtained a more structured and
simpler proof of one of the main results
in
[
18
]
, which states that the coinductive
generalized resumption monad transformer preserves Elgotness.

Theorem
5.10
characterizes iteratable Elgot
monads as iteration-congruent retracts of their

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-transforms. We take this perspective further as
follows.

Definition 5.16
.

We extend the notation
F^{\nu}=\nu\gamma.F(-+\gamma)
to
functors
F
. We say that a functor
F
is

•

1
-iteratable
if
F^{\nu}
exists,

•

(n+1)
-iteratable
if
F^{\nu}
is
n
-iteratable,

•

\omega
-iteratable
if
F
is
n
-iteratable for every
n
.

We apply all these notions mainly to monads
\mathbb{T}
, referring to their
underlying functor
T
.

Remark 5.17
.

Note that for every natural number
n
,

\displaystyle\nu\gamma^{\prime}.\,\nu\gamma.\,T(X+\gamma^{\prime}+n\times\gamma)\;\mathbin{\cong}\nu\gamma.\,T(X+\gamma+n\times\gamma)\;\mathbin{\cong}\nu\gamma.\,T(X+(n+1)\times\gamma),

where
n\times X
denotes the
n
-fold sum
X+\ldots+X
. It follows by
induction that
n
-iteratability of
T
is equivalent to the
assumption that all coalgebras
\nu\gamma.\,T(X+n\times\gamma)
exist,
a condition that does not appear much stronger than iteratability
of
T
. Still, the
2
-iteratable functors are properly contained in
the iteratable functors, as the following example shows. Let
\mathbf{C}
be
the category of countable sets and
T=\operatorname{Id}
. Then, it is easy to see
that
T^{\nu}X
is isomorphic to
X\times\mathbb{N}+1
, hence
\operatorname{Id}
is
iteratable. However, it is not
2
-iteratable, because

(T^{\nu})^{\nu}\iobj\cong\nu\gamma.\,2\times\gamma
can be
characterized as the object of all infinite bit streams, which does
not fit into
\mathbf{C}
for cardinality reasons. Showing this formally
amounts to mimicking Cantor’s classical diagonalization argument.

We expect that separating
n
-iteratability from
(n+1)
-iteratability
for
n>1
would involve much less natural examples, as the previous
cardinality argument typically would not apply.

Consider the functor
\mathbb{T}\mapsto\mathbb{T}^{\nu}
on the category
of
\omega
-iteratable monads over

\mathbf{C}
. This construction is itself a monad: the unit
\bm{\eta}

is the natural transformation with components

\bm{\eta}_{X}=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\colon TX\to T^{\nu}X
, and the multiplication

\bm{\mu}\colon T^{\nu\nu}\to T^{\nu}
has components

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{X}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\colon T^{\nu\nu}X\to T(X+T^{\nu\nu}X)\bigr).

We record explicitly that the relevant laws are satisfied:

Lemma 8
.

With multiplication
\bm{\mu}
and unit
\bm{\eta}
as defined above, the
construction
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
becomes a monad on the (overlarge)
category of
\omega
-iteratable monads.

Proof 5.18
.

By coinduction. Using the definitions of
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{\eta}
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\mu}\bm{\eta}=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(T^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(T^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,T\bigl(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+(T^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\,(T^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\eta}\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},

and therefore
\bm{\mu}\bm{\eta}=\mathsf{id}
by uniqueness of final coalgebra
morphisms. Analogously,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\mu}\bm{\eta}^{\nu}=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,

\displaystyle T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}T(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,

\displaystyle T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\eta}^{\nu})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

and therefore
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\mu}\bm{\eta}^{\nu}=\mathsf{id}
. The remaining
law
\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}=\bm{\mu}\,\bm{\mu}^{\nu}
follows by the same argument
from

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\nu}=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle T\bigl(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\bigr)\,\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu})\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}^{\nu})\,\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\nu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}^{\nu})]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\nu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}^{\nu})\,\bm{\mu}]\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;
