\displaystyle T[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\nu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\nu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}^{\nu})\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(T^{\nu}[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu})\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}T^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu})\,T^{\nu}[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}T^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu})]\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}T^{\nu}[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T[(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu})\,[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}],\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}T^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu})\,T^{\nu}[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]]\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T[(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu})\,[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}],\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu})\,T[[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}],\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle T(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\bm{\mu})\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,T[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

For every
T
we now define the
delay transformation

\operatorname{\rhd}=\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\colon T^{\nu}\to T^{\nu}.

This leads to our second main result:

Theorem 5.19
.

The category of
\omega
-iteratable Elgot monads over
\mathbf{C}
is
isomorphic to the full subcategory of the category of

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras consisting of the

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras
(\mathbb{S},\rho\colon\mathbb{S}^{\nu}\to\mathbb{S})
(for
\omega
-iteratable
\mathbb{S}
) satisfying
{\rho\operatorname{\rhd}=\rho}
.

We refer to the condition
{\rho\operatorname{\rhd}=\rho}
as
delay
cancellation
.

Remark 9
.

The point of the above result is to systematize the connection
between the
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
construction and Elgot monads
previously indicated by
Theorem
5.10
. Alternative efforts to show that
Elgotness is monadic exist (see Section
7
) but
necessarily involve quite different monads than
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
:
Any monad
\mathfrak{M}
(on a category of monads) whose algebras are
precisely the Elgot monads would itself have to produce Elgot monads

\mathfrak{M}\mathbb{T}
, while the point of involving
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
is to obtain
Elgot monads from guarded iterative ones.

Formally, the following simple example shows that the delay
cancellation condition
\rho\operatorname{\rhd}=\rho
cannot be omitted from
Theorem
5.19
. Let
\mathbf{Mon}(\mathbf{C})^{\nu}
be the
category of
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras, and let

\mathbf{Mon}(\mathbf{C})^{\nu}_{\operatorname{\rhd}}
be the full subcategory of

\mathbf{Mon}(\mathbf{C})^{\nu}
figuring in
Theorem
5.19
. Since the identity functor is the
initial monad, the initial object of
\mathbf{Mon}(\mathbf{C})^{\nu}
is
Capretta’s delay monad
[
7
]

D=\nu\gamma.\,(\operatorname{-\!-}+\gamma)
. On the other hand, the initial
object of
\mathbf{Mon}(\mathbf{C})^{\nu}_{\operatorname{\rhd}}
(if it exists) is the

initial Elgot monad

\mathbb{L}
, which on
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{Set}
is the

maybe monad

(\operatorname{-\!-})+1
.

If
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{Set}
, then
DX=(X\times\mathbb{N}+1)
does turn out to be
Elgot
[
15
]
(but applying
Theorem
5.19
to
D
qua Elgot monad yields a
different
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebra structure than the initial one),
and
\mathbb{L}
is, in this case, a retract of
\mathbb{D}
in

\mathbf{Mon}(\mathbf{C})^{\nu}_{\operatorname{\rhd}}
. The situation is more intricate in categories with a nonclassical
internal logic, for which
\mathbb{D}
is mainly intended. We believe that
in such a setting, neither is
\mathbb{D}
Elgot in general, nor is
\mathbb{L}

the maybe monad. However, there will still be a unique

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebra morphism
\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{L}
in

\mathbf{Mon}(\mathbf{C})^{\nu}
.

Proof 5.20

(Proof (Theorem
5.19
))
.

We fix the notation
(\eta,\operatorname{-\!-}^{\star},\dagger)
for (potential) Elgot monads over

\mathbf{C}
and
(\eta^{\nu},\operatorname{-\!-}^{\text{\kreuz}},\ddagger)
for their
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-transforms.
We record the following identity, satisfied by any monad morphism
\rho
for which

\rho\bm{\eta}=\mathsf{id}
and
\rho\operatorname{\rhd}=\rho
:

\displaystyle\rho=[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}.

(25)

Indeed,

\displaystyle[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\rho\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/
\rho\bm{\eta}=\mathsf{id}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho\operatorname{\rhd}]^{\star}\rho\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/
\rho\operatorname{\rhd}=\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,[\eta,\operatorname{\rhd}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\bm{\eta}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/
\rho
is a monad morphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\,[\eta,\operatorname{\rhd}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\,[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta,\mathsf{id}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta,\mathsf{id}]^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}\,[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho.

For the inclusion from Elgot monads to
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras, let

\mathbb{S}
be an Elgot monad. By Theorem
5.10
,

\mathbb{S}
is an iteration-congruent retract of
\mathbb{S}^{\nu}
with

S^{\nu}X=\nu\gamma.\,S(X+\gamma)
; specifically,

\upsilon=\operatorname{\rhd}\bm{\eta}\colon S\to S^{\nu}
is a left inverse to
\rho=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\colon S^{\nu}\to S
.

First of all, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\rho\operatorname{\rhd}=[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\rhd}=[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}=\rho.

Moreover, we need to show the axioms of
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras:

\displaystyle\rho\bm{\eta}=\mathsf{id}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho\bm{\mu}=\rho\rho^{\nu}

(26)

where
\rho^{\nu}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\colon S^{\nu\nu}\to S^{\nu}
. For the left axiom, we
readily have
\mathsf{id}=\rho\upsilon=\rho\operatorname{\rhd}\bm{\eta}=\rho\bm{\eta}
. The right axiom is
shown as follows:

\displaystyle\rho\bm{\mu}

\displaystyle\;\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(i)}{=\joinrel=}\rho[\eta,\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(ii)}{=\joinrel=}\rho\bigl(\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)^{\ddagger}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle(iii)}{=\joinrel=}\rho\rho^{\nu}.

To show step (i), first observe that on the one hand

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(S[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

Indeed, let
t=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(S[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,t\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=

\displaystyle\;S(\mathsf{id}+t)\,S[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S[\mathsf{id}+t\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},t\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S(\mathsf{id}+\,t\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})\,S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},

which means that
\,t\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
satisfies the equation uniquely characterizing

\bm{\mu}
, hence
\bm{\mu}=t\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
. On the other hand,
[\eta,\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}

satisfies the equation characterizing
t
. In order to see this, note that

\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}=\;\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},

(27)

witnessed by the following calculation:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}(

\displaystyle\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\eta^{\nu},\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\,(\mathsf{id}+[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}),\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

/​​/ (
27
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\,(\mathsf{id}+[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}})\,[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S(\mathsf{id}+[\eta^{\nu},\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}})\,S[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}.

In summary we obtain

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}\bigl(S[\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bigr)\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=[\eta^{\nu},(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},

which justifies (i). Let us check (iii). Let us denote
\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
by
t
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}t^{\ddagger}=\;

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},t^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}[\eta^{\nu},t^{\ddagger}],\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}[\eta^{\nu},t^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta^{\nu}\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\eta^{\nu},\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}t^{\ddagger}]^{\star}\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle S(\mathsf{id}+t^{\ddagger})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}},

and therefore
t^{\ddagger}
satisfies the equation characterizing
\rho^{\nu}
, hence

\rho^{\nu}=t^{\ddagger}
. Finally, we proceed with the proof of (ii). Using the fact
that
\rho
is a monad morphism and that it cancels
\operatorname{\rhd}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\rho[\eta,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\rho\operatorname{\rhd}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho[\eta,\,(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}]^{\text{\kreuz}}\operatorname{\rhd}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho(\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\ddagger}.

/​​/ fixpoint

In order to finish the proof of (ii), it suffices to check that

\displaystyle\rho\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=\rho\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

(28)

and call the assumption that
\rho
is an iteration congruence. The proof
of (
28
) runs as follows:

\displaystyle\rho\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,\rho]^{\star}S(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}+\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2})\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1},\rho\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\rho\operatorname{\rhd}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}.

We have thus proved the claimed inclusion on objects. To extend the claim to morphisms, suppose that
\alpha\colon\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{T}
is an Elgot monad morphism, i.e. a monad morphism
such that
\alpha f^{\dagger}=(\alpha f)^{\dagger}
, and let us show that it is also
a morphism of the corresponding
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras, i.e.
\alpha\rho=\rho\alpha^{\nu}
.
Indeed, on the one hand
\alpha\rho=\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}=(\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\dagger}
, and also
on the other hand, by uniformity of
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
,
\rho\alpha^{\nu}=\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\dagger}\alpha^{\nu}=(\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})^{\dagger}
, since
\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\alpha^{\nu}=T(\mathsf{id}+\alpha^{\nu})\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}
.

We proceed with the converse inclusion, i.e. from

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras to Elgot monads. So assume that

(\mathbb{S},\rho)
is a
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebra, i.e. the
laws (
26
) are satisfied, and
\rho\operatorname{\rhd}=\rho
. We claim
that
\mathbb{S}
equipped with the iteration operation

f^{\dagger}=\rho(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)
is an Elgot monad. The corresponding axioms
are verified as follows.

•

Fixpoint.
Let
f\colon X\to S(Y+X)
. Then
f^{\ddagger}=\rho(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)
and
hence

\displaystyle f^{\ddagger}=\;

\displaystyle\rho(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle[\eta,\rho]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle[\eta,\rho]^{\star}S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)f

/​​/ (
25
)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle[\eta,\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle[\eta,f^{\ddagger}]^{\star}f.

•

Naturality.
Let
f\colon X\to S(Y+X)
and
g\colon Y\to SZ
. Then

\displaystyle g^{\star}f^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle\;g^{\star}\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)=\rho\,(\bm{\eta}g)^{\text{\kreuz}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f),

\displaystyle([(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\,f)^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle\;\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\,f).

We are left to show that
(\bm{\eta}g)^{\text{\kreuz}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)
satisfies the equation for

\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}([(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\,f)
. This runs as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}(\bm{\eta}g)^{\text{\kreuz}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)=

\displaystyle\;[\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\eta}\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}(\bm{\eta}g)^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}(\bm{\eta}g)^{\text{\kreuz}}]^{\star}S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}(\bm{\eta}g)^{\text{\kreuz}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)]^{\star}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S(\mathsf{id}+(\bm{\eta}g)^{\text{\kreuz}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))\,[(S\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})\,g,\eta\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]^{\star}\,f.

•

Codiagonal.
Let
f\colon X\to S((Y+X)+X)
. Observe that since

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,\rho\,S^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))\,\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)

/​​/ naturality of
\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~S(\mathsf{id}+(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)))\,\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f),

we have that

\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))=(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))

(29)

by uniqueness of final morphisms. Thus,

\displaystyle f^{\dagger\dagger}=\;

\displaystyle\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)))

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\rho\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}))(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))

/​​/ (
29
)

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\rho\,\rho^{\nu}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))

/​​/ definition of
\rho^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\rho\,\bm{\mu}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)).

/​​/ (
26
)

Since by definition,
(S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f)^{\dagger}=\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f)
, we are only
left to check that
\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f)=\bm{\mu}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))
. This is easy to
establish directly by showing that the right-hand side satisfies the equation
characterizing the left-hand side:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\bm{\mu}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu})\,S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,S^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))\,\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,S((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))+S^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)))\,\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\bm{\mu}\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}]\,S((\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))+S^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)))\,S(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\,\bm{\mu}\,\operatorname{\operatorname{\mathsf{out}}^{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\kern-0.5pt1}\kern-1.0pt}S^{\nu}(\mathsf{id}+\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)]\,f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S[\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)),\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\,\bm{\mu}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f))]\,f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S(\mathsf{id}+\bm{\mu}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)))\,S[\mathsf{id},\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}]\,f.

•

Uniformity.
Let
f\colon X\to T(Y+X)
,
g\colon Z\to S(Y+Z)
,
h\colon Z\to X

and suppose that
f\,h=T(\mathsf{id}+h)\,g
. If follows standardly by uniqueness of final coalgebra morphisms that
(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)\,h=\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g
and therefore

\displaystyle f^{\ddagger}\,h=\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)\,h=\rho\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}g)=g^{\ddagger}.

Finally, let us check that every
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebra morphism
\alpha\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}

is an Elgot monad morphism. By assumption we have that
\alpha\rho=\rho\alpha^{\nu}
,
and therefore, for every
f\colon X\to S(Y+X)
,
\alpha f^{\dagger}=\alpha\rho(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)=\rho\alpha^{\nu}(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)=\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)
. It is then
straightforward to verify that
\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\alpha\operatorname{\mathsf{out}})(\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}f)=\rho\operatorname{\mathsf{coit}}(\alpha f)=(\alpha f)^{\dagger}
.

6.
A Sandwich Theorem for Elgot Monads

As an application of Theorem
5.10
, we show that
sandwiching an Elgot monad between a pair of adjoint functors again
yields an Elgot monad. A similar result has previously been shown for
completely iterative monads
[
31
]
; this result
generalizes straightforwardly to guarded iterative monads:

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
F\colon\mathbf{C}\to\mathbf{D}
and
U\colon\mathbf{D}\to\mathbf{C}
be a pair of adjoint
functors with associated natural isomorphism

\Phi\colon\mathbf{D}(FX,Y)\to\mathbf{C}(X,UY)
, and let
\mathbb{T}
be a guarded
iterative monad on
\mathbf{D}
. Then the monad induced on the composite
functor
UTF
is guarded iterative, with the guardedness relation
defined by taking
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}UTFY
if and only if

\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f\colon FX\to_{\sigma}TFY
, and unique solutions given by

f\mapsto\Phi((\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)^{\dagger})
.

Proof 6.2
.

First, we need to verify that the guardedness relation defined in
the claim satisfies the rules from Definition
3.1
. Note
that since left adjoints preserve coproducts (LAPC), we can assume
w.l.o.g. that
F(X+Y)=FX+FY
.

•

(trv)
Let
f\colon X\to UTFY
be a morphism. By
(trv)
for
\mathbb{T}
, we have
(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)\colon FX\to_{\sigma}T(FY+FX)
. Then, the following holds:

\displaystyle(T\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)=

\displaystyle\;(TF\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)

/​​/ LAPC

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((UTF\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f)

/​​/
\Phi
is a natural isomorphism

Thus,
\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((UTF\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f)\colon FX\to_{\sigma}T(FY+FX)
, so

(UTF\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1})f\colon X\to_{\sigma}UTF(Y+X)
.

•

(par)
Let
f\colon X\to_{\sigma}UTFZ
and
g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}UTFZ
. This means that
\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f\colon FX\to_{\sigma}TFZ
and
\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g\colon FY\to_{\sigma}TFZ
, hence, by
(par)
for
\mathbb{T}
,
[\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f,\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g]\colon FX+FY\to_{\sigma}TFZ
. By LAPC, we have
[\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f,\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g]=\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}[f,g]\colon F(X+Y)\to_{\sigma}TFZ
, so
[f,g]\colon X+Y\to_{\sigma}UTFZ
.

•

(cmp)
Let
f\colon X\to_{2}UTF(Y+Z)
,
g\colon Y\to_{\sigma}UTFV
, and
h\colon Z\to UTFV
be morphisms. Then, by
(cmp)
for
\mathbb{T}
, we obtain that
[\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g,\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}h]^{\star}(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)\colon FX\to_{\sigma}TFV
. Then, the following holds:

\displaystyle\;[\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g,\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}h]^{\star}(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mu^{\mathbb{T}}(T[\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g,\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}h])(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mu^{\mathbb{T}}(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}(UT[\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g,\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}h])f)

/​​/
\Phi
is a nat. iso.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((U\mu^{\mathbb{T}})(UT[\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g,\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}h])f)

/​​/
\Phi
is a nat. iso.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((U\mu^{\mathbb{T}})(UT\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}[g,h])f)

/​​/ LAPC

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((U\mu^{\mathbb{T}})(UT\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}\mathsf{id})(UTF[g,h])f)

/​​/
\Phi
is a nat. iso.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}(\mu^{UTF}(UTF[g,h])f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}([g,h]^{\star}f).

Thus,
\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}([g,h]^{\star}f)\colon X\to_{\sigma}UTF(Y+X)
in

\mathbb{T}
, so
[g,h]^{\star}f\colon FX\to_{\sigma}TF(Y+X)
in the monad on
UTF
.

This means that if
f\colon X\to_{2}UTF(Y+X)
, then

\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f\colon FX\to_{2}T(FY+FX)
, so
\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f
has a unique
solution due to the fact that
\mathbb{T}
is guarded iterative. The rest of
the proof is the same as for Theorem 3.1 in
[
31
]
.

Now, to obtain a similar result for Elgot monads, we can
easily combine Theorems
5.10

and
6.1
without having to verify the equational
properties by hand.

Theorem 6.3
.

With an adjunction as in Theorem
6.1
, let
\mathbb{S}

be an Elgot monad on
\mathbf{D}
. Then, the monad induced on the
composite
USF
is an Elgot monad.

Proof 6.4
.

By Theorem
5.10
, there exist a guarded
iterative monad
\mathbb{T}
and an iteration-congruent retraction

\rho\colon\mathbb{T}\to\mathbb{S}
. By Theorem
6.1
, the monad
induced on
UTF
is guarded iterative. Thus, it is enough to show that

U\rho F:UTF\to USF
is an iteration-congruent retraction.

•

It is a retraction, since retractions are preserved by all functors.

•

To see that it is guarded, let
f\colon X\to USFY
be

\sigma
-guarded. By definition, this means that

\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f\colon FX\to SFY
is
\sigma
-guarded in
\mathbb{S}
. Since

\rho
is a guarded retraction, it follows that

\upsilon(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)
, for
\rho
’s family of sections
\upsilon
,
is also
\sigma
-guarded. By the fact that
\Phi
is a natural
isomorphism, we obtain

\upsilon(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)=\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((U\upsilon)f)
, hence, by
definition,
(U\upsilon)f
is also
\sigma
-guarded.

•

To see that
U\rho F
is an iteration congruence, let us denote
by
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\dagger}
the solution in
\mathbb{T}
, and by

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\ddagger}
the solution in the monad on
UTF
. Let

f,g\colon X\to_{2}UTF(X+Y)
be morphisms such that

(U\rho)f=(U\rho)g
. First, using this and the fact that
\Phi
is
a natural isomorphism, we obtain the following:

\rho(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)=\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((U\rho)f)=\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}((U\rho)g)=\rho(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g)

Thus, by the fact that
\rho
is an iteration congruence, we obtain that
\rho(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)^{\dagger}=\rho(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g)^{\dagger}
. Now, we check that
U\rho F
is an iteration congruence:

\displaystyle(U\rho)f^{\ddagger}=

\displaystyle\;(U\rho)(\Phi(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi(\rho(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}f)^{\dagger})

/​​/
\Phi
is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\Phi(\rho(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g)^{\dagger})

/​​/ the above

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(U\rho)(\Phi(\Phi^{{\text{\kern 0.5pt-}\mathsf{\kern-0.5pt1}}}g)^{\dagger})

/​​/
\Phi
is a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(U\rho)g^{\ddagger}

Example 6.5

(From Metric to CPO-based Iteration)
.

As an example exhibiting sandwiching as well as the setting of
Theorem
5.10
, we compare two iteration
operators on
\mathbf{Set}
that arise from different fixed point
theorems: Banach’s, for complete metric spaces, and Kleene’s, for
complete partial orders, respectively. We obtain the first operator
by sandwiching Escardo’s
metric lifting
monad

\mathbb{S}

[
12
]
in the adjunction between sets and
bounded complete ultrametric spaces (which forgets the metric in one
direction and takes discrete spaces in the other), obtaining a monad

\bar{\mathbb{S}}
on
\mathbf{Set}
. Given a bounded complete metric space
(X,d)
,

S(X,d)
is a metric on the set
(X\times\mathbb{N})\cup\{\bot\}
. As
we show in the appendix,
\mathbb{S}
is guarded iterative if we define

f\colon(X,d)\to S(Y,d^{\prime})
to be
\sigma
-guarded if
k>0
whenever

f(x)=(\sigma(y),k)
. By Theorem
6.1
,

\bar{\mathbb{S}}
is also guarded iterative (of course, this can also be
shown directly). The second monad arises by sandwiching the identity
monad on cpos with bottom in the adjunction between sets and cpos
with bottom that forgets the ordering in one direction and adjoins
bottom in the other, obtaining an Elgot monad
\mathbb{L}
on Set
according to Theorem
6.3
. The latter is
unsurprising, of course, as
\mathbb{L}
is just the maybe monad
LX=X+1
.

The monad
\bar{\mathbb{S}}
keeps track of the number of steps needed to
obtain the final result. We have an evident extensional collapse map

\rho\colon\bar{\mathbb{S}}\to\mathbb{L}
, which just forgets the number of steps. One
can show that
\rho
is in fact an iteration-congruent retraction,
so we obtain precisely the situation of
Theorem
5.10
. Technical details are in the
appendix.

7.
Related Work

Alternatively to our guardedness relation on Kleisli morphisms,
guardedness can be formalized using type constructors
[
28
]

or, categorically, functors, as in
guarded fixpoint
categories

[
24
]
. Roughly speaking, in such settings
a morphism
X\to Y+Z
is guarded in
Z
if it factors through a
morphism
X\to Y+\blacktriangleright Z
where
\blacktriangleright
is
a functor or type constructor to be thought of as isolating the
guarded inhabitants of a type. The functorial approach, giving rise to

guarded fixpoint categories
, covers also total guardedness,
like we do. Our approach is slightly more fine-grained, and in
particular natively supports the two variants of the dinaturality
axiom (Figure
2
), which, e.g., in guarded fixpoint
categories require additional assumptions
[
24
, Proposition
3.15]
akin to the one we discuss in
Remark
3
. In our own subsequent work, we have
generalized the notion of abstract guardedness from co-Cartesian to
symmetric monoidal categories
[
17
]
, where
guardedness becomes a more symmetric concept: among morphisms

X\otimes Y\to Z\otimes W
, where
\otimes
is the monoidal structure,
one distinguishes morphisms that are (simultaneously)
unguarded

in the input
A
and
guarded
in the output
D
.

A result that resembles our Theorem
5.19
, due to
Adámek et al.
[
3
]
, states roughly that if

\mathbf{C}
is locally finitely presentable and hyperextensive (a property
imposing certain compatibility constraints between pullbacks and
countable coproducts, satisfied, e.g., over sets and over complete
partial orders), then the finitary Elgot monads are the algebras for a
monad on the category of endofunctors given by

H\mapsto L_{H}=\rho\gamma.\,(\operatorname{-\!-}+1+H\gamma)
where
\rho
takes

rational fixpoints
(i.e. final coalgebras among those where
every point generates a finite subcoalgebra); that is, in the
mentioned setting, finitary Elgot monads are monadic over
endofunctors. Besides Theorem
5.19
making fewer
assumptions on
\mathbf{C}
, the key difference (indicated already in
Remark
9
) is that, precisely by dint of the mentioned
result,
L_{H}
is already a finitary Elgot monad (namely, the free
finitary Elgot monad over
H
); contrastingly, we characterize Elgot
monads as quotients of
guarded iterative
monads, i.e. of
monads where guarded recursive definitions have
unique

fixpoints.

8.
Conclusions and Further Work

We have given a unified account of monad-based guarded and unguarded
iteration by axiomatizing the notion of guardedness to cover standard
definitions of guardedness, and additionally, as a corner case, what
we call
total guardedness
, i.e. the situation when all
morphisms are declared to be guarded. We thus obtain a common umbrella
for
guarded iterative monads
, i.e. monads with unique iterates
of guarded morphisms, and Elgot monads, i.e. totally guarded monads
satisfying Elgot’s classical laws of iteration. We reinforce the view
that the latter constitute a canonical model for monad-based unguarded
iteration by establishing the following equivalent characterizations:
Provided requisite final coalgebras exist, a monad
\mathbb{T}
is Elgot iff
it satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions:

•

it satisfies the quasi-equational theory of iteration
[
2
,
18
]
(definition);

•

it is an iteration-congruent retract of a guarded iterative monad (Theorem
5.10
);

•

it is an algebra
(\mathbb{T},\rho)
of the monad
T\mapsto\nu\gamma.\,T(X+\gamma)
in
the category of monads satisfying a natural delay cancellation condition (Theorem
5.19
).

In future work, we aim to investigate further applications of this
machinery, in particular to examples which did not fit previous
formalizations. One prospective target is suggested by work of Nakata
and Uustalu
[
29
]
, who give a coinductive big-step
trace semantics for a while-language. We conjecture that this work has
an implicit guarded iterative
monad
\mathbb{T}
\mathbb{R}
under the
hood, for which guardedness cannot be defined using the standard
argument based on a final coalgebra structure of the monad because

\mathbb{T}
\mathbb{R}
is not a final
coalgebra. Moreover, we aim to extend the treatment of iteration in
finite trace semantics via iteration-congruent retractions
(Example
5.12
) to infinite traces, possibly taking
orientation from recent work on coalgebraic infinite trace
semantics
[
34
]
.

In type theory, there is growing interest in forming an extensional
quotient of the delay
monad
[
9
,
4
]
. It is shown
in
[
9
]
that under certain reasonable
conditions, a suitable collapse of the delay monad by removing delays
is again a monad; however, the proof is already quite complex, and
proving directly that the collapse is in fact an Elgot monad, as one
would be inclined to expect, seems daunting. We expect that
Theorem
5.19
may shed light on this issue. A natural
question that arises in this regard is whether the subcategory of

(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras figuring in the theorem is reflexive. A
positive answer would provide a means of constructing canonical
quotients of
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\nu}
-algebras (such as the delay monad) with
the results automatically being Elgot monads.

Appendix A
Details of Example
6.5

As an example of the setting described in
Theorem
5.10
, we compare two iteration operators
on
\mathbf{Set}
that arise from different fixed point theorems:
Banach’s for complete metric spaces and Kleene’s for complete partial
orders, respectively. The first operator keeps track of the number of
steps needed to obtain the final result. We show that its extensional
collapse, defined as a morphism that forgets the number of steps, is
an iteration-congruent retraction with the second operator as the
retract.

First, we consider the category
\mathbf{CbUMet}
of complete
1
-bounded
ultrametric spaces and nonexpansive maps. Note that
\mathbf{CbUMet}
has
coproducts and is Cartesian closed
[
33
]
. Following
Escardó
[
12
]
, one has a monad
T
on
\mathbf{CbUMet}

given on objects as

T(A,d)=((A\times\mathbb{N})\cup\{\infty\},d^{\prime})
, with
d^{\prime}

given by

\displaystyle d^{\prime}(\infty,\infty)=0

\displaystyle d^{\prime}((x,k),\infty)=d^{\prime}(\infty,(x,k))=(1/2)^{k}

\displaystyle d^{\prime}((x,k),(y,k))=(1/2)^{k}d(x,y)

\displaystyle d^{\prime}((x,k),(y,t))=(1/2)^{\min(k,t)}\text{ if $k\neq t$}

The monad structure on
T
is defined as expected, by

\eta(a)=(a,0)

and

f^{\star}(a,k)=(b,k+t)\text{ where $f(a)=(b,t)$}

Theorem A.1
.

The monad
T
is guarded iterative with
f\colon X\to TY
being
\sigma
-guarded if for all
x
and
y
,
f(x)=(\sigma(y),k)
implies
k>0
.

Proof A.2
.

First, note that the product of
({A,d_{A}})
and
({B,d_{B}})
in
\mathbf{CbUMet}
is given by
({A\times B,d_{A\times B}})
, where

d_{A\times B}(({x_{1},y_{1}}),({x_{2},y_{2}}))=\max\{d_{A}(x_{1},x_{2}),d_{B}(y_{1},y_{2})\}.

The exponential object is equal to
({B^{A},d_{A\Rightarrow B}})
where

d_{A\Rightarrow B}(f,g)=\sup\{d_{B}(f(x),g(x))\ |\ x\in A\}.

The coproduct is given by
({A+B,d_{A+B}})
, where

d_{A+B}(p,q)=\begin{cases}d_{A}(x_{1},x_{2})&\text{if }p=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\,x_{1}\text{ and }q=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}\,x_{2}\\
d_{B}(y_{1},y_{2})&\text{if }p=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\,y_{1}\text{ and }q=\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}\,y_{2}\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Now, we show that the monad
\mathbb{T}
is guarded. The only nontrivial case is
(cmp)
. So, assume
([g,h]^{\star}f)(x)=(\sigma(y),k)
. We consider two cases:

•

f(x)=(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}z,k^{\prime})
. Then, since
f
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}
-guarded,
k^{\prime}>0
, so, by definition of
(\operatorname{-\!-})^{\star}
,
k>0
.

•

f(x)=(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}z,k^{\prime})
. Then,
([g,h]^{\star}f)(x)=[g,h]^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}z,k^{\prime})=(\sigma(y),k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime})
, where
g(z)=(\sigma(y),k^{\prime\prime})
. Since
g
is
\sigma
-guarded,
k^{\prime\prime}>0
, so
k=k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}>0
.

Given a guarded morphism
f\colon X\to_{1}T(Y+X)
, we define the morphism
f^{\dagger}\colon X\rightarrow TY
as the unique fixed point of the following map
\psi:(X\rightarrow TY)\rightarrow(X\rightarrow TY)
:

\displaystyle\psi(g)

\displaystyle=[\eta,g]^{\star}f

One can easily see that any fixed point of
\psi
satisfies the fixed point identity, and that the uniqueness of such a fixed point gives us that
f
has a unique solution. We use Banach’s theorem to achieve both.

By Banach’s theorem, it is enough to show that
\psi
is contractive, that is, there exists a non-negative real
c<1
such that for maps
g,g^{\prime}\colon X\rightarrow TY
, the following holds:

d_{X\Rightarrow TY}(\psi(g),\psi(g^{\prime}))\leq c\cdot d_{X\Rightarrow TY}(g,g^{\prime})

(30)

The left-hand side of the equation (
30
) is equal to:

\displaystyle d_{X\Rightarrow TY}(\psi(g),\psi(g^{\prime}))=\sup\{d_{TY}(\psi(g)(x),\psi(g^{\prime})(x))\ |\ x\in X\}

In turn, the right-hand side is as follows:

\displaystyle c\cdot d_{X\Rightarrow TY}(g,g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=c\cdot\sup\{d_{TY}(g(x),g^{\prime}(x))\ |\ x\in X\}

\displaystyle=\sup\{c\cdot d_{TY}(g(x),g^{\prime}(x))\ |\ x\in X\}

Thus, it is enough to show that for all
x\in X
, there exists
y\in X
such that:

d_{TY}(\psi(g)(x),\psi(g^{\prime})(x))\leq c\cdot d_{TY}(g(y),g^{\prime}(y))

We show this for
c=1/2
. We consider two cases:

•

f(x)=(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}y,k)
for some
y\in Y
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then, for all
g\colon X\to TY
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\psi(g)(x)=

\displaystyle\;([\eta,g]^{\star}f)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,g]^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{1}y,k)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(y,k)

So, the following holds:

\displaystyle d_{TY}(\psi(g)(x),\psi(g^{\prime})(x))=

\displaystyle\;d_{TY}((y,k),(y,k))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;0

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;(1/2)\cdot d_{TY}(g(x),g^{\prime}(x))

•

f(x)=(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}y,k+1)
for some
y\in X
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
(the ‘
+1
’ part follows from the fact that
f
is guarded). Assume that
g(y)=(z,t)
for some
z
and
t
. Then

\displaystyle\psi(g)(x)=

\displaystyle\;([\eta,g]^{\star}f)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;[\eta,g]^{\star}(\operatorname{\mathsf{in}}_{2}y,k+1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(z,t+k+1).

Similarly, let
g^{\prime}(y)=(z^{\prime},t^{\prime})
, and so
\psi(g^{\prime})(x)=(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}+k+1)
. Then, it follows that:

\displaystyle d_{TY}(\psi(g)(x),\psi(g^{\prime})(x))=

\displaystyle\;d_{TY}((z,t+k+1),(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}+k+1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(1/2)^{k+1}\cdot d_{TY}((z,t),(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(1/2)^{k}\cdot(1/2)\cdot d_{TY}((z,t),(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\;(1/2)\cdot d_{TY}((z,t),(z^{\prime},t^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;(1/2)\cdot d_{TY}(g(y),g^{\prime}(y))

We thus obtain a monad
U_{\mathrm{D}}TF_{\mathrm{D}}
on
\mathbf{Set}
by
sandwiching
T
in the adjunction
F_{\mathrm{D}}\dashv U_{\mathrm{D}}
where
U_{\mathrm{D}}
is the
forgetful functor
\mathbf{CbUMet}\to\mathbf{Set}
and
F_{\mathrm{D}}
takes discrete metrics. By
Theorem
6.1
,
U_{\mathrm{D}}TF_{\mathrm{D}}
is guarded iterative.

For the second operator, let
\mathbf{Cpo}_{\bot}
be the category of complete
partial orders and continuous bottom-preserving functions. The
identity on
\mathbf{Cpo}_{\bot}
is an Elgot monad, hence, by
Theorem
6.3
, we obtain an Elgot monad
U_{\mathrm{L}}F_{\mathrm{L}}

on
\mathbf{Set}
by sandwiching in the adjunction
F_{\mathrm{L}}\dashv U_{\mathrm{L}}
where
U_{\mathrm{L}}

is the forgetful functor
\mathbf{Cpo}_{\bot}\to\mathbf{Set}
and
F_{\mathrm{L}}
adjoins bottom. The
relation between the two monads on
\mathbf{Set}
is an instance of our notion
of iteration-congruent retraction:

Theorem A.3
.

Define
\rho\colon U_{\mathrm{D}}TF_{\mathrm{D}}\to ULF_{\mathrm{L}}
by
\rho(a,k)=a
and

\rho(\infty)=\bot
. Then
\rho
is an iteration-congruent
retraction with the section given by
\upsilon(a)=(a,1)
and

\upsilon(\bot)=\infty
. Moreover, the respective iteration
operators induced by
\rho
and the sandwich theorem coincide.

Proof A.4
.

It is trivial that
\rho
is a guarded retraction. To see that it is a iteration
congruence, we first define an auxiliary relation: given two functions
f,h\colon X\to(B\times\mathbb{N})\cup\{\infty\}
, we write
f\sim h
if
f(x)=(a,k)
for some
a\in B
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
if and only if
h(x)=(a,k^{\prime})
for some
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
and
f(x)=\infty
if and only if
h(x)=\infty
(i.e. the two functions differ only in the number of steps needed to obtain the value). We also write
\psi_{f}(g)=[\eta,g]^{\star}f
for the function
\psi
from the proof of Theorem
A.1
.

Given a
2
-guarded function
f\colon X\to((Y+X)\times\mathbb{N})\cup\{\infty\}
, the function
f^{\dagger}
can be defined as the unique fixed point of
\psi_{f}
(see the proof of Theorem
A.1
), which by Banach’s fixed-point theorem is given by the limit of the sequence
W^{f}_{0}=c
and
W^{f}_{(n+1)}=\psi_{f}(W^{f}_{n})
, where
c
is the constant function
c(x)=\infty
. It is easy to see that for each
x
the sequence
W^{f}_{n}(x)
stabilizes. Given a function
h
such that
f\sim h
, it is easy to show by induction that for every
x
, the sequence
W^{f}_{n}(x)
stabilizes with
(a,k)
for some
k
if and only if
W^{h}_{n}(x)
stabilizes with
(a,k^{\prime})
for some
k^{\prime}
at the same index
n
. Then, for all
x
,
f^{\dagger}(x)=(a,k)
and
h^{\dagger}(x)=(a,k^{\prime})
, so
\rho(f^{\dagger}(x))=\rho(h^{\dagger}(x))
. Then, the result is obtained by noticing that for all
f
and
h
,
\rho f=\rho h
implies
f\sim h
.

It is left to see that the solution operator that follows from the sandwich theorem and the one that follows from the iteration-congruent retraction coincide. Given
f\colon X\to(X+Y)\cup\{\bot\}
, its solution in the Elgot monad
U_{\mathrm{L}}F_{\mathrm{L}}
is given by the fixed point of the equation
\phi(g)=[\eta,g]^{\star}f
, that is, by Kleene’s theorem, by the limit of the sequence
W^{\prime}_{0}=c^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}_{(n+1)}=\phi_{f}(W^{\prime}_{n})
, where
c^{\prime}
is the constant function
c^{\prime}(x)=\bot
. It is easy to see that
W^{\prime}_{n}=\rho W^{(\upsilon f)}_{n}
, so the solutions coincide.

Forgetting the provenance of the above-mentioned monads on

\mathbf{Set}
via sandwiching, we obtain that the maybe monad
(\operatorname{-\!-})+\{\bot\}

on
\mathbf{Set}
is an iteration-congruent retract of the delay monad

(\operatorname{-\!-})\times\mathbb{N}+\{\bot\}
, which is, of course, not surprising. In
categories beyond sets (where the delay monad, or partiality monad, is
more generally defined as
\nu\gamma.(-+\gamma)

[
7
]
),
the situation is more complex, see Remark
9
.
